Grade 2 English — every answer key

295 questions: 195 comprehension, 100 quiz. Nothing here is machine-verifiable — Cambridge publishes no Stage 1–4 answer booklet for English.

Read these 190 first. Their answer is an open template, or they carry no options, so no checker can say anything about them at all. Answer each question yourself from the text before you look at the key — a key that is wrong in the same way its explanation is wrong is invisible to any other method.

Not checkable by anything (190)

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq001 · unit 1 · Short answer · Words Around Us

The text names four places where we can see words. What are they?

answer: In books, on tablets, on signs and at home.

Leo names the four places one at a time: “I can see words in books”, “on tablets”, “on signs” and “at home”.

the text it is asked about
On Saturday morning, Leo woke up and looked around his room. “Words are everywhere,” he said to himself, and he decided to go on a word hunt.
First, Leo picked up his favourite book from the shelf. The cover said The Clever Fox in big red letters. “I can see words in books,” said Leo, and he read the title out loud.
Next, Leo's mother called him to the kitchen. She was looking at a tablet on the table. “Look, Leo,” she said. “This tablet shows the weather for today.” Leo read the word SUNNY on the screen. “I can see words on tablets too,” he said happily.
After breakfast, Leo walked to the market with his mother to buy bread. On the way, he saw a big sign outside a shop. It said BAKERY in tall blue letters. “I can see words on signs,” said Leo, pointing at the sign. His mother smiled. “You are right. Signs help us find places.”
When they got home, Leo looked around his own house. On the fridge there was a note from his father. On his bedroom door there was a sign that said LEO'S ROOM. “I can see words at home too,” said Leo. “Words really are all around us!”
That afternoon, Leo told his friend Nora about his word hunt. “I found words in books, on tablets, on signs and at home,” he said. “Where else do you think we can find words?”
Nora thought for a moment. “What about on our clothes, or on food packets?” she said. “Words are everywhere we look!”
Leo smiled. “You are right, Nora. And every time we read a word, we learn something new. Words help us learn!”
That night, Leo told his mother about all the words he had found that day. “I found so many words,” he said proudly. “In books, on tablets, on signs, and at home.”
His mother hugged him. “What a wonderful word hunt,” she said. “Tomorrow, let's look for even more.”

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq002 · unit 1 · Short answer · Words Around Us

Near the end of the text, Leo tells Nora why words matter. What does he say that words do?

answer: It says that words help us learn.

The text says “Words help us learn!”, which gives the reason.

the text it is asked about
On Saturday morning, Leo woke up and looked around his room. “Words are everywhere,” he said to himself, and he decided to go on a word hunt.
First, Leo picked up his favourite book from the shelf. The cover said The Clever Fox in big red letters. “I can see words in books,” said Leo, and he read the title out loud.
Next, Leo's mother called him to the kitchen. She was looking at a tablet on the table. “Look, Leo,” she said. “This tablet shows the weather for today.” Leo read the word SUNNY on the screen. “I can see words on tablets too,” he said happily.
After breakfast, Leo walked to the market with his mother to buy bread. On the way, he saw a big sign outside a shop. It said BAKERY in tall blue letters. “I can see words on signs,” said Leo, pointing at the sign. His mother smiled. “You are right. Signs help us find places.”
When they got home, Leo looked around his own house. On the fridge there was a note from his father. On his bedroom door there was a sign that said LEO'S ROOM. “I can see words at home too,” said Leo. “Words really are all around us!”
That afternoon, Leo told his friend Nora about his word hunt. “I found words in books, on tablets, on signs and at home,” he said. “Where else do you think we can find words?”
Nora thought for a moment. “What about on our clothes, or on food packets?” she said. “Words are everywhere we look!”
Leo smiled. “You are right, Nora. And every time we read a word, we learn something new. Words help us learn!”
That night, Leo told his mother about all the words he had found that day. “I found so many words,” he said proudly. “In books, on tablets, on signs, and at home.”
His mother hugged him. “What a wonderful word hunt,” she said. “Tomorrow, let's look for even more.”

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq003 · unit 1 · Short answer · Words Around Us

Look around your own home. Name two places where you can see words that this text does not name.

answer: Any two real places that are not in the text, such as on a food packet, a shop sign, a shirt label or a television screen.

The child must first notice which four places the text already names, then add new ones of their own.

the text it is asked about
On Saturday morning, Leo woke up and looked around his room. “Words are everywhere,” he said to himself, and he decided to go on a word hunt.
First, Leo picked up his favourite book from the shelf. The cover said The Clever Fox in big red letters. “I can see words in books,” said Leo, and he read the title out loud.
Next, Leo's mother called him to the kitchen. She was looking at a tablet on the table. “Look, Leo,” she said. “This tablet shows the weather for today.” Leo read the word SUNNY on the screen. “I can see words on tablets too,” he said happily.
After breakfast, Leo walked to the market with his mother to buy bread. On the way, he saw a big sign outside a shop. It said BAKERY in tall blue letters. “I can see words on signs,” said Leo, pointing at the sign. His mother smiled. “You are right. Signs help us find places.”
When they got home, Leo looked around his own house. On the fridge there was a note from his father. On his bedroom door there was a sign that said LEO'S ROOM. “I can see words at home too,” said Leo. “Words really are all around us!”
That afternoon, Leo told his friend Nora about his word hunt. “I found words in books, on tablets, on signs and at home,” he said. “Where else do you think we can find words?”
Nora thought for a moment. “What about on our clothes, or on food packets?” she said. “Words are everywhere we look!”
Leo smiled. “You are right, Nora. And every time we read a word, we learn something new. Words help us learn!”
That night, Leo told his mother about all the words he had found that day. “I found so many words,” he said proudly. “In books, on tablets, on signs, and at home.”
His mother hugged him. “What a wonderful word hunt,” she said. “Tomorrow, let's look for even more.”

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq004 · unit 1 · Short answer · A Poem: “When I Open Up a Book”

In the poem, what does each page whisper when you open a book?

answer: Each page whispers, “Look! Look! Look!”

The second line of the poem gives the exact words that the page whispers.

the text it is asked about
When I open up a book
Each page whispers, “Look! Look! Look!”
Sports and monkeys, trains and kings,
Stories of amazing things.

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq005 · unit 1 · Short answer · A Poem: “When I Open Up a Book”

The third line of the poem lists four things you can read about. Name them, then say which one you would choose.

answer: Sports, monkeys, trains and kings. Then accept any one of the four with a reason for the choice.

The first part is straight recall from line three. The second part asks you to say what you like and why.

the text it is asked about
When I open up a book
Each page whispers, “Look! Look! Look!”
Sports and monkeys, trains and kings,
Stories of amazing things.

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq006 · unit 1 · Short answer · Meeting a Partner

Who is Maya's partner, and what does that partner like?

answer: Maya's partner is Nora, and Nora likes trees.

Maya gives her partner's name in the second sentence and says what she likes in the third.

the text it is asked about
Listen to this story about Maya and her new partner, Nora.
It was the first day of partner work in Teacher Yasmin's class. “Everybody, please choose a partner for today,” said Teacher Yasmin.
Maya looked around the room. She saw a girl sitting quietly near the window. “Would you like to be my partner?” Maya asked.
The girl smiled. “Yes, please,” she said. “My name is Nora.”
“Hello, Nora! My name is Maya,” said Maya. “Let's tell the class about each other.”
When it was their turn, Maya stood up next to Nora. “This is my partner,” Maya said. “Her name is Nora. She likes trees. She is my friend.”
The class clapped, and Teacher Yasmin smiled. “Well done, Maya. Now, Nora, can you tell us about Maya?”
Nora nodded. “This is my partner,” she said. “Her name is Maya. She likes singing. She is my friend too.”
At break time, Maya and Nora sat together under a big tree in the playground. “Why do you like trees so much, Nora?” asked Maya.
“I like trees because they are tall and quiet, and birds live in them,” said Nora. “What do you like about singing?”
“I like singing because it makes me happy,” said Maya. “My mother sings with me every night before bed.”
Leo walked over and sat down with them. “Can I be your friend too?” he asked.
“Of course!” said Maya and Nora together.
“What do you like, Leo?” asked Nora.
“I like football,” said Leo. “And I like mangoes best of all!”
The three friends laughed and talked until the bell rang for class.
That afternoon, Teacher Yasmin asked the class, “What is one new thing you learned about your partner today?”
Maya put up her hand first. “I learned that Nora likes trees because birds live in them,” she said.
Nora put up her hand next. “I learned that Maya loves singing with her mother,” she said.
“Wonderful,” said Teacher Yasmin. “When we listen carefully to our partner, we learn something new every time.”
On the way home, Maya told her big sister about her new partner. “Her name is Nora,” said Maya. “She likes trees, and she is my friend now.”
Her sister smiled. “That sounds like a lovely partner,” she said. “Maybe next week you can invite her to visit.”
Maya nodded happily. She could not wait for the next partner activity at school.

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq007 · unit 1 · Short answer · Meeting a Partner

Does Maya say “he” or “she” when she talks about Nora? Why does she choose that word?

answer: She says “she”, because Nora is a girl. She also uses “her” when she says “Her name is Nora”.

This links the listening text to Grammar practice 1: she is used for a girl or a woman.

the text it is asked about
Listen to this story about Maya and her new partner, Nora.
It was the first day of partner work in Teacher Yasmin's class. “Everybody, please choose a partner for today,” said Teacher Yasmin.
Maya looked around the room. She saw a girl sitting quietly near the window. “Would you like to be my partner?” Maya asked.
The girl smiled. “Yes, please,” she said. “My name is Nora.”
“Hello, Nora! My name is Maya,” said Maya. “Let's tell the class about each other.”
When it was their turn, Maya stood up next to Nora. “This is my partner,” Maya said. “Her name is Nora. She likes trees. She is my friend.”
The class clapped, and Teacher Yasmin smiled. “Well done, Maya. Now, Nora, can you tell us about Maya?”
Nora nodded. “This is my partner,” she said. “Her name is Maya. She likes singing. She is my friend too.”
At break time, Maya and Nora sat together under a big tree in the playground. “Why do you like trees so much, Nora?” asked Maya.
“I like trees because they are tall and quiet, and birds live in them,” said Nora. “What do you like about singing?”
“I like singing because it makes me happy,” said Maya. “My mother sings with me every night before bed.”
Leo walked over and sat down with them. “Can I be your friend too?” he asked.
“Of course!” said Maya and Nora together.
“What do you like, Leo?” asked Nora.
“I like football,” said Leo. “And I like mangoes best of all!”
The three friends laughed and talked until the bell rang for class.
That afternoon, Teacher Yasmin asked the class, “What is one new thing you learned about your partner today?”
Maya put up her hand first. “I learned that Nora likes trees because birds live in them,” she said.
Nora put up her hand next. “I learned that Maya loves singing with her mother,” she said.
“Wonderful,” said Teacher Yasmin. “When we listen carefully to our partner, we learn something new every time.”
On the way home, Maya told her big sister about her new partner. “Her name is Nora,” said Maya. “She likes trees, and she is my friend now.”
Her sister smiled. “That sounds like a lovely partner,” she said. “Maybe next week you can invite her to visit.”
Maya nodded happily. She could not wait for the next partner activity at school.

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq008 · unit 1 · Short answer · The Days of the Week Song

How many days does the song say make one week?

answer: Seven days make one week.

The last line of the song answers the question twice: “Seven days make one week — seven days in all!”

the text it is asked about
Sing along:
“Saturday, Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday. Seven days make one week — seven days in all!”

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq009 · unit 1 · Short answer · The Days of the Week Song

Which day does the song sing straight after Wednesday?

answer: Thursday comes straight after Wednesday.

The song lists the days in order, so you can find Wednesday and read on by one day.

the text it is asked about
Sing along:
“Saturday, Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday. Seven days make one week — seven days in all!”

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq010 · unit 1 · Short answer · Amal's First Week

How does Amal spell her name for Teacher Yasmin?

answer: She says the letters A-M-A-L.

Amal spells her name aloud near the start of the story, and it makes her feel braver.

the text it is asked about
It was Amal's first day at a new school. The school was near the market in her town. Amal held her mother's hand and looked at the big blue door.
“Good morning,” said a kind teacher. “Welcome! My name is Teacher Yasmin. What is your name?”
“My name is Amal,” she said in a small voice.
“Can you spell it for me, Amal?” asked Teacher Yasmin.
“A-M-A-L,” said Amal. She felt a little braver now.
Inside the classroom, the children sat in a circle. On the wall there was a big calendar and a chart of colours. A boy with a yellow cap smiled at Amal.
“Hello! My name is Leo,” he said. “Today is Saturday. At our school, Saturday is the first day of the week. You can be my partner!”
“Thank you, Leo,” said Amal. She liked him already.
Teacher Yasmin pointed to the calendar. “Let us say the days of the week together,” she said. The whole class sang: “Saturday, Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday!”
“Now,” said Teacher Yasmin, “tell your partner one thing you like.”
Amal turned to Leo. “I like drawing,” she said. “What do you like?”
“I like football,” said Leo. “And I like mangoes! My birthday is in the month of June, when the mangoes are sweet.”
Amal laughed. “My birthday is in December,” she said. “I will be seven years old.”
All week, Amal learned something new. On Monday she learned her colours. On Tuesday she counted to twelve. On Wednesday she read her first English book, about a clever fox.
On Thursday, a new girl came to the class. She stood by the door and looked shy, just like Amal on her first day.
Amal remembered how kind Leo had been. She walked over and smiled.
“Hello,” said Amal. “My name is Amal. What is your name? You can be my partner today.”
The new girl smiled back. “My name is Nora,” she said. “Thank you.”
Teacher Yasmin watched and nodded. “Well done, Amal,” she said softly. “A good friend makes everybody feel welcome.”
That night, Amal told her mother about her week. “I have two friends now,” she said happily. “Leo and Nora. And I know all the days of the week!”
Her mother hugged her. “You had a wonderful first week,” she said. “Tomorrow is Friday, a day to rest.”
Amal closed her eyes and smiled. She could not wait for Saturday, when a brand new school week would begin.

Which day is the first day of the school week in this story?

answer: Saturday is the first day of the school week.

Leo says it on Amal's first morning, and the class then sings the days in that order.

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq013 · unit 1 · Short answer · Amal's First Week

In which month is Leo's birthday, and in which month is Amal's?

answer: Leo's birthday is in June and Amal's birthday is in December.

Leo links his birthday to sweet mangoes in June, and Amal answers with her own month.

What kind thing did Amal do for Nora on Thursday, and why did she do it?

answer: She said hello and asked Nora to be her partner, because she remembered how kind Leo had been to her.

The story says that Amal remembered Leo's kindness first, and then walked over to the new girl.

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq015 · unit 1 · Short answer · Amal's First Week

How did Amal feel on her first morning, and how had she changed by Thursday? Use the story to explain.

answer: At first she spoke in a small voice and felt shy. By Thursday she felt brave enough to welcome a new girl. Two new friends and a kind teacher helped her to change.

A good answer gives evidence from the story: the small voice at the start, and the welcome that Amal gives on Thursday.

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq016 · unit 1 · Short answer · Amal's First Week

Teacher Yasmin said, “A good friend makes everybody feel welcome.” What is one thing you could say or do to welcome a new child?

answer: Any kind, practical idea, such as saying hello, asking the new child's name, offering to be their partner or sharing a book.

A strong answer names one action and says how it would help the new child to feel welcome.

the text it is asked about
It was Amal's first day at a new school. The school was near the market in her town. Amal held her mother's hand and looked at the big blue door.
“Good morning,” said a kind teacher. “Welcome! My name is Teacher Yasmin. What is your name?”
“My name is Amal,” she said in a small voice.
“Can you spell it for me, Amal?” asked Teacher Yasmin.
“A-M-A-L,” said Amal. She felt a little braver now.
Inside the classroom, the children sat in a circle. On the wall there was a big calendar and a chart of colours. A boy with a yellow cap smiled at Amal.
“Hello! My name is Leo,” he said. “Today is Saturday. At our school, Saturday is the first day of the week. You can be my partner!”
“Thank you, Leo,” said Amal. She liked him already.
Teacher Yasmin pointed to the calendar. “Let us say the days of the week together,” she said. The whole class sang: “Saturday, Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday!”
“Now,” said Teacher Yasmin, “tell your partner one thing you like.”
Amal turned to Leo. “I like drawing,” she said. “What do you like?”
“I like football,” said Leo. “And I like mangoes! My birthday is in the month of June, when the mangoes are sweet.”
Amal laughed. “My birthday is in December,” she said. “I will be seven years old.”
All week, Amal learned something new. On Monday she learned her colours. On Tuesday she counted to twelve. On Wednesday she read her first English book, about a clever fox.
On Thursday, a new girl came to the class. She stood by the door and looked shy, just like Amal on her first day.
Amal remembered how kind Leo had been. She walked over and smiled.
“Hello,” said Amal. “My name is Amal. What is your name? You can be my partner today.”
The new girl smiled back. “My name is Nora,” she said. “Thank you.”
Teacher Yasmin watched and nodded. “Well done, Amal,” she said softly. “A good friend makes everybody feel welcome.”
That night, Amal told her mother about her week. “I have two friends now,” she said happily. “Leo and Nora. And I know all the days of the week!”
Her mother hugged her. “You had a wonderful first week,” she said. “Tomorrow is Friday, a day to rest.”
Amal closed her eyes and smiled. She could not wait for Saturday, when a brand new school week would begin.

eng-g02-t01-u01-cq017 · unit 1 · Short answer · Amal's First Week

Amal learned something new on each day of her week. Which day of your own week do you enjoy most, and what do you do on that day?

answer: Any day named correctly, with one activity that really happens on it. The day name should be written with a capital letter.

This links the story week to your own week and practises the day names in a real sentence.

the text it is asked about
It was Amal's first day at a new school. The school was near the market in her town. Amal held her mother's hand and looked at the big blue door.

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq001 · unit 2 · Short answer · Firefighters and Their Equipment

What two jobs does the text say firefighters do?

answer: They put out fires, and they rescue people who are trapped inside burning buildings.

Both jobs are named in the text, one after the other.

the text it is asked about
Teacher Yasmin’s class visited the fire station on a sunny Tuesday morning. Amal, Theo and Nora held hands as they walked through the big red doors.
“Firefighters have some of the most important jobs in our neighbourhood,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Let’s find out what they do.”
A firefighter named Leila met them beside a shiny red fire engine. “Welcome!” she said. “Would you like to know what a firefighter’s job really looks like?”
“Yes, please!” said the children together.
“Our first job is to put out fires,” said Leila. “When smoke rises over the market or over a house, we hurry there with water and hoses.”
“What is your second job?” asked Theo.
“Our second job is just as important,” said Leila. “Sometimes people are trapped inside a burning building. We rescue them and carry them to safety.”
Amal looked at the equipment hanging by the fire engine. “Why do you wear so many things?” she asked.
“Every piece keeps me safe,” said Leila. She pointed to her feet. “These are my boots. They are heavy and strong, so hot ground cannot hurt my feet.”
She tapped her head. “This is my helmet. It protects my head from falling things.”
Then she held up a mask. “This mask covers my nose and mouth. It lets me breathe fresh air, even when the room is full of smoke.”
Last, she pulled on a thick pair of gloves. “These gloves are thick enough to hold something hot without burning my hands.”
Nora tried on a spare helmet, and it wobbled over her eyes. Everyone laughed.
“So a firefighter puts out fires and rescues people,” said Amal slowly, “and wears boots, a helmet, a mask and gloves to stay safe while doing it.”
“Exactly right,” said Leila, smiling. “You were listening carefully.”
Before the class left, Leila let each child sit inside the fire engine for a moment and ring the bell once.
“Thank you, Leila,” said Teacher Yasmin. “We will remember everything you taught us today.”
On the bus back to school, Amal drew a picture of a firefighter in her notebook, carefully labelling the helmet, the mask, the gloves and the boots.

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq003 · unit 2 · Short answer · Firefighters and Their Equipment

Why does a firefighter need a mask, and why does she need gloves?

answer: The mask lets her breathe fresh air inside the smoke, and the gloves let her hold hot things without burning her hands.

The text gives both reasons: fresh air for the mask, and hot things for the gloves.

the text it is asked about
Teacher Yasmin’s class visited the fire station on a sunny Tuesday morning. Amal, Theo and Nora held hands as they walked through the big red doors.

She tapped her head. “This is my helmet. It protects my head from falling things.”

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq004 · unit 2 · Short answer · Firefighters and Their Equipment

Draw and label a firefighter. Beside each label, write the part of the body that the equipment keeps safe.

answer: Helmet keeps the head safe, mask covers the face for breathing, gloves protect the hands, and boots protect the feet.

Every label must come from the text, and each one must be joined to the right part of the body.

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq005 · unit 2 · Short answer · A Poem: “My Neighbourhood”

Which five kinds of people does the poem say live in the neighbourhood?

answer: Grandmas, grandpas, mums, dads and children.

Lines three and four of the poem list all five together.

the text it is asked about
Come and meet the people in my neighbourhood.
There are neighbours helping neighbours in my neighbourhood.
There are grandmas and grandpas,
Mums and dads, children too,
In my neighbourhood, the streets around my home.

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq006 · unit 2 · Short answer · A Poem: “My Neighbourhood”

What does the poem say the neighbours are doing?

answer: The neighbours are helping other neighbours.

Line two of the poem says that neighbours are helping neighbours.

the text it is asked about
Come and meet the people in my neighbourhood.
There are neighbours helping neighbours in my neighbourhood.
There are grandmas and grandpas,
Mums and dads, children too,
In my neighbourhood, the streets around my home.

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq007 · unit 2 · Short answer · A Poem: “My Neighbourhood”

Which three words are repeated in the poem, and what new line could you add about your own street?

answer: The words in my neighbourhood are repeated. A new line could be: There are helpers waving in my neighbourhood.

Spotting the repeated phrase shows how the poem is built, and the new line must keep the same pattern.

the text it is asked about
Come and meet the people in my neighbourhood.
There are neighbours helping neighbours in my neighbourhood.
There are grandmas and grandpas,
Mums and dads, children too,
In my neighbourhood, the streets around my home.

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq008 · unit 2 · Short answer · An Interview with a Firefighter

Leila is a ______.

answer: firefighter

Leila says this herself in the text.

the text it is asked about
Teacher Yasmin’s class had visited the fire station last month, and Amal still remembered every piece of Leila’s equipment. This week, Teacher Yasmin had exciting news. “Firefighter Leila is coming to visit us today,” she said. Amal and her classmates could hardly wait.
Just before lunch, a tall woman in a blue uniform walked into the classroom. She smiled and said, “Hello again, everyone! Do you remember me?”
“Yes!” said Amal. “You are Leila, the firefighter!”
Teacher Yasmin asked the children to take turns asking questions, and Amal put up her hand first.
“What is your job?” asked Amal.
“I am a firefighter,” said Leila. “I work at the fire station near the market.”
A boy named Theo asked the next question. “What happens when there is a fire?”
“When there is a fire, the alarm rings and I ride on the fire engine as fast as we can go,” said Leila. “I use water to fight the fire and keep everyone safe.”
Nora leaned forward. “Are you ever scared?” she asked.
Leila thought for a moment. “Sometimes,” she said, “but I remember my training, and I trust my team. We always work together.”
“What do you do when there is no fire?” asked Amal.
“That is my favourite question,” said Leila, laughing. “I clean the fire engine, I check the hoses, and I visit schools like this one to talk to children about fire safety. I want every child to know what to do if there is ever a fire at home.”
“What should we do?” asked Theo.
“Get out quickly, stay low under the smoke, and never go back inside for toys,” said Leila. “Then find a grown-up or call for help.”
At the end of the visit, Leila let the children try on her helmet again. It still felt heavy, and Amal could hardly see over the top of it.
“Thank you for visiting us, Leila,” said Teacher Yasmin.
“Thank you for your excellent questions,” said Leila. “Remember, firefighters are your neighbours too. If you ever need us, we will come.”
That evening, Amal told her family all about it. “I want to visit the fire station again one day,” she said, “and see the fire engine for myself.”

She also visits ______ and talks to children about ______.

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq022 · unit 2 · Short answer · The Helpers of Warta Street

When Omar thanks his neighbours, he wipes his eyes. Why do you think he does that?

answer: He is so happy and thankful that tears come to his eyes.

The story does not say that Omar cries. It says he wipes his eyes while he says “Thank you, my good neighbours” - so a reader works out that he has tears of thanks. That is a meaning the words show without saying it.

the text it is asked about
Amal lived on Warta Street, near the market. She loved her street because everyone helped each other. The people who lived near her were her neighbours, and they were kind.
One hot morning, Amal sat by her window. She watched the busy street.
“Look, Mum,” she said. “The window cleaner is here!”
A tall man named Karim was cleaning the windows of the shop next door. He used a long pole to reach the high glass.
“Good morning, Amal!” Karim called. “What are you doing today?”
“I am watching all the helpers,” said Amal. “Who else works on our street?”
Karim smiled. “Many good people work here,” he said. “Look, here comes the bus driver.”
A big blue bus stopped at the corner. The bus driver, Nadia, waved. She wore a neat blue uniform.
“Hello, Amal!” said Nadia. “I am driving to the city. Do you want to count my passengers?”
Amal laughed and counted. “One, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight, nine, ten! There are ten people on your bus!”
Just then, a loud bell rang. Amal saw smoke behind the market.
“A fire!” shouted Karim. “Everyone, stay back!”
A red fire engine came fast down the street. Two firefighters jumped out. They wore helmets on their heads, heavy boots on their feet, and thick gloves on their hands. One firefighter, Leila, wore a mask on her face so that she could breathe.
“What are they doing?” asked Amal.
“They are putting out the fire,” said her mother. “And look, they are rescuing the baby goats!”
The firefighters used water to fight the fire. Soon the smoke was gone. Everyone on Warta Street cheered.
A police officer named Rami arrived on his bicycle. He kept the people safe.
“Please stand back, my friends,” said Rami kindly. “The market is safe now. Thank you for helping each other.”
That is when Amal saw old Omar. Omar sold bread and fruit in the market. His small shop was full of smoke, and he looked sad.
“Mum, we must help him,” said Amal.
So Amal and her mother ran to help. Karim brought water. Nadia swept the floor. Rami carried the heavy boxes. Even the two firefighters helped clean the shop.
“Thank you, my good neighbours,” said Omar, wiping his eyes. “You all have important jobs, but today you helped an old shopkeeper. That is the kindest job of all.”
Omar gave everyone fresh bread. It was warm and soft.
That night, Amal told her father about the day. “A window cleaner, a bus driver, two firefighters, a police officer,” she said. “Everyone on Warta Street helps.”
“Yes,” said her father. “And so do you. A good neighbour is the best helper of all.”
Amal smiled and closed her eyes. Tomorrow she would sit by her window again, and maybe help a neighbour too.

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq013 · unit 2 · Short answer · The Helpers of Warta Street

On which street does Amal live, and what stands close to her home?

answer: Amal lives on Warta Street, and the market stands close to her home.

The very first sentence of the story gives the street and the market.

the text it is asked about
Amal lived on Warta Street, near the market. She loved her street because everyone helped each other. The people who lived near her were her neighbours, and they were kind.
One hot morning, Amal sat by her window. She watched the busy street.
“Look, Mum,” she said. “The window cleaner is here!”
A tall man named Karim was cleaning the windows of the shop next door. He used a long pole to reach the high glass.
“Good morning, Amal!” Karim called. “What are you doing today?”
“I am watching all the helpers,” said Amal. “Who else works on our street?”
Karim smiled. “Many good people work here,” he said. “Look, here comes the bus driver.”
A big blue bus stopped at the corner. The bus driver, Nadia, waved. She wore a neat blue uniform.
“Hello, Amal!” said Nadia. “I am driving to the city. Do you want to count my passengers?”
Amal laughed and counted. “One, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight, nine, ten! There are ten people on your bus!”
Just then, a loud bell rang. Amal saw smoke behind the market.
“A fire!” shouted Karim. “Everyone, stay back!”
A red fire engine came fast down the street. Two firefighters jumped out. They wore helmets on their heads, heavy boots on their feet, and thick gloves on their hands. One firefighter, Leila, wore a mask on her face so that she could breathe.
“What are they doing?” asked Amal.
“They are putting out the fire,” said her mother. “And look, they are rescuing the baby goats!”
The firefighters used water to fight the fire. Soon the smoke was gone. Everyone on Warta Street cheered.
A police officer named Rami arrived on his bicycle. He kept the people safe.
“Please stand back, my friends,” said Rami kindly. “The market is safe now. Thank you for helping each other.”
That is when Amal saw old Omar. Omar sold bread and fruit in the market. His small shop was full of smoke, and he looked sad.
“Mum, we must help him,” said Amal.
So Amal and her mother ran to help. Karim brought water. Nadia swept the floor. Rami carried the heavy boxes. Even the two firefighters helped clean the shop.
“Thank you, my good neighbours,” said Omar, wiping his eyes. “You all have important jobs, but today you helped an old shopkeeper. That is the kindest job of all.”
Omar gave everyone fresh bread. It was warm and soft.
That night, Amal told her father about the day. “A window cleaner, a bus driver, two firefighters, a police officer,” she said. “Everyone on Warta Street helps.”
“Yes,” said her father. “And so do you. A good neighbour is the best helper of all.”
Amal smiled and closed her eyes. Tomorrow she would sit by her window again, and maybe help a neighbour too.

eng-g02-t01-u02-cq018 · unit 2 · Short answer · The Helpers of Warta Street

Why did Omar call helping him the kindest job of all?

answer: Because the helpers had already done their own paid jobs that day, and they chose to help him only out of kindness.

Omar contrasts their important jobs with the free choice they made to help one old shopkeeper.

Amal's father said that a good neighbour is the best helper of all. What does he mean?

answer: He means that anyone can notice a need and help kindly, and that helping does not have to be part of a paid job.

Link his words back to the moment when the whole street cleaned Omar's shop for nothing.

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq001 · unit 3 · Short answer · A Poem: “Reach for the Sky!”

Which action words does the poem tell you to do? List them all.

answer: Reach, clap, touch, turn, put, flap, jump and wiggle.

The poem gives eight commands in order, one or two in every line. Reading each line slowly helps you find them all.

the text it is asked about
Read the poem and do each action as you say it:
Reach for the sky!
Clap your hands, touch your toes.
Turn around. Put your finger on your nose.
Flap your arms, jump up high.
Wiggle your fingers and reach for the sky!

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq002 · unit 3 · Short answer · A Poem: “Reach for the Sky!”

Which body parts does the poem name? List them all.

answer: Hands, toes, finger, nose, arms and fingers.

Hands and toes are in line two, finger and nose in line three, arms in line four, and fingers in the last line.

the text it is asked about
Read the poem and do each action as you say it:
Reach for the sky!
Clap your hands, touch your toes.
Turn around. Put your finger on your nose.
Flap your arms, jump up high.
Wiggle your fingers and reach for the sky!

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq003 · unit 3 · Short answer · A Poem: “Reach for the Sky!”

Which line of the poem is said at the beginning and then again at the end?

answer: “Reach for the sky!”

Poems often repeat a line. Here the poem opens with “Reach for the sky!” and the last line finishes with the same words.

the text it is asked about
Read the poem and do each action as you say it:
Reach for the sky!
Clap your hands, touch your toes.
Turn around. Put your finger on your nose.
Flap your arms, jump up high.
Wiggle your fingers and reach for the sky!

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq004 · unit 3 · Short answer · Healthy and Strong

Which three activities does the text name as great ways to keep fit and healthy?

answer: Walking, running and cycling.

The text lists all three activities together.

the text it is asked about
On Monday morning, Teacher Yasmin held up a poster with a big red heart on it. “Who can tell me a way to keep fit and healthy?” she asked.
Sami put up his hand first. “Walking!” he said. “I walk to school every day with my big sister.”
“Running,” said Nora. “I run around the field at break time.”
“Cycling,” said Leo. “I ride my bicycle to the park on Saturdays.”
“Wonderful,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Walking, running and cycling are all great ways to keep fit and healthy. Moving your body every day helps you stay strong.”
Amal raised her hand slowly. “But what about food, Teacher Yasmin? Does that help too?”
“Yes, it does,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Eating healthy food gives you energy to play and learn. Fruit, vegetables and brown bread are all healthy foods.”
Theo grinned. “I had porridge and a banana for breakfast today. I feel like I could run all the way around the field!”
“That is exactly right,” said Teacher Yasmin, laughing. “Now, who can guess my next clue? It is clear, and you drink it.”
“Water!” shouted the whole class together.
“Yes! Drinking water helps your body work well,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Your body needs water every single day, especially after you exercise.”
Nora looked thoughtful. “My mum always tells me to drink water after PE.”
“Your mum is right,” said Teacher Yasmin. “And my last clue is about the night, not the day. What do we do at night to help our bodies?”
“Sleep!” said Sami.
“Getting enough sleep helps you grow,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Boys and girls your age need about ten hours of sleep every night.”
Leo counted on his fingers. “I go to bed at ten and wake up at seven. Is that enough?”
“Almost,” smiled Teacher Yasmin. “Try going to bed just a little earlier tonight.”
At the end of the lesson, Teacher Yasmin pointed back at the poster. “So remember: move every day, eat healthy food, drink water, and sleep well. Then your body will be healthy and strong, just like all of you!”
The whole class cheered and clapped their hands.

According to the text, what does moving your body every day help you to do?

The text says that moving your body every day helps you stay strong.

the text it is asked about

“Cycling,” said Leo. “I ride my bicycle to the park on Saturdays.”
“Wonderful,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Walking, running and cycling are all great ways to keep fit and healthy. Moving your body every day helps you stay strong.”

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq007 · unit 3 · Short answer · Healthy and Strong

The text mentions water and sleep. What does each one do for your body?

answer: Drinking water helps your body work well, and getting enough sleep helps you grow.

Two separate sentences give the two answers, so a full answer names both.

On Monday morning, Teacher Yasmin held up a poster with a big red heart on it. “Who can tell me a way to keep fit and healthy?” she asked.

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq008 · unit 3 · Short answer · “Get Up and Move” Day

What is the special day at school called in this listening text?

answer: “Get Up and Move” day.

The text names the day.

the text it is asked about
Listen carefully to this text about a special day at school. You can listen again as many times as you like, and read the words on the screen too.
Today is “Get Up and Move” day at Amal's school. Every year, the whole school stops its normal lessons for one morning and goes outside to move.
“Good morning, everyone!” called Teacher Yasmin, standing on the school field. “Today, all the children are doing different activities. Choose a corner and get moving!”
In the first corner, Sami and Leo are jumping over small hoops on the grass. “Jump higher, Leo!” laughs Sami, as they bounce from hoop to hoop.
In the next corner, Nora is hopping along a chalk path, one foot at a time. “I can hop all the way to the end without stopping,” she says proudly.
Near the school gate, some children are waving flags in the “Get Up and Move” parade. Amal waves her flag high above her head, and her little brother waves back from the fence.
By the music corner, other children are nodding their heads and clapping their hands in time with the drum. Theo nods so fast that his cap nearly falls off, and everyone laughs.
Everyone is having fun while moving their bodies. Some children are jumping and hopping. Others are waving their hands and nodding their heads. Nobody is sitting still today!
At the end of the morning, Teacher Yasmin gathers the whole school together. “Well done, everyone,” she says. “Moving helps us stay healthy and strong. I hope you enjoyed our ‘Get Up and Move’ day.”
Amal smiles and looks at her tired legs. “That was the best morning ever,” she says to Nora. “Can we do it again tomorrow?”
Teacher Yasmin laughs. “Not tomorrow — but we can do a little moving every single day, even a small dance in the kitchen before school!”

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq009 · unit 3 · Short answer · “Get Up and Move” Day

Name at least three movements that the children in the text are doing.

answer: Any three of: jumping, hopping, waving (flags or hands), nodding their heads, clapping their hands.

The text names five movements, so any three of them answer fully.

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq010 · unit 3 · Short answer · “Get Up and Move” Day

The text ends by saying why moving matters. What does moving help us to be?

answer: Healthy and strong.

The text says that moving helps us stay healthy and strong.

the text it is asked about

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq019 · unit 3 · Short answer · The Big Race

Before the race ends, which clue tells you that Amal might help Theo?

answer: She cheered for Leo when he wobbled, so she is kind to others.

Early in the story Amal shouts “Keep going, Leo!” when he nearly falls. A reader who has seen her be kind can predict the ending: she walks back and helps Theo stand up.

the text it is asked about
It was sports day at Amal's school. The sun was warm and the field was full of children. Everyone was ready to run, jump and hop.
“Drink your water,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Move your bodies and do your best. A strong body is a healthy body!”
Amal was in the green team with Nora, Sami and Leo. Theo was in the red team. Theo could run very fast, and he wanted everyone to know.
“My legs are the fastest legs in the school,” said Theo. He waved his arms and jumped up high. “The red team will win!”
“Do not worry,” Nora said to her team. She nodded her head. “We will run together and do our best.”
The first race was the hopping race. The children lined up. Teacher Yasmin raised her hand.
“Ready, steady, go!” she called.
The children hopped on one foot across the field. Leo was hopping fast, but he wobbled and nearly fell. “Keep going, Leo!” shouted Amal, clapping her hands. Leo did not give up. He hopped all the way to the line.
Next came the big relay race. In a relay, the runners take turns. Each runner passes a stick to the next one. Amal was the last runner for the green team.
“Ready, steady, go!” called Teacher Yasmin again.
Nora ran fast. Sami ran faster. Then Leo passed the stick to Amal. But Amal's tummy felt full of butterflies. Her legs felt slow.
“I am running as fast as I can,” Amal puffed. “But Theo is winning!”
Theo was far in front. He was busy waving to the crowd. He did not look at his feet. Suddenly he tripped and fell onto the grass. His stick rolled away.
Amal ran past him. Her team was cheering. “Go, Amal, go!” they shouted, clapping and jumping. Amal reached the line first. The green team had won!
Everyone cheered and waved. But Amal looked back. Theo was still sitting on the grass. He was rubbing his knee, and he looked very sad.
Amal did not jump for joy. She walked back to Theo and put out her hand.
“Are you hurt?” she asked kindly.
“Only a little,” said Theo. “But I lost. And I said so many silly things.”
Amal smiled. “You are still the fastest runner in the school,” she said. “Come on. Stand up.”
She helped Theo stand. They walked back together, and all the children clapped for them.
Teacher Yasmin nodded and smiled. “Well done, everyone,” she said. “Amal, you ran your best and you never gave up. And you were kind to Theo. That is the true winner.”
That evening, Amal told her mother about the big race. “I won,” she said, “but the best part was helping Theo. Next time, we will be on the same team!”
Her mother hugged her. “You have a fast body and a kind heart,” she said. “Now drink your water and rest. Tomorrow is another day to move and play.”

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq011 · unit 3 · Short answer · The Big Race

What special day is it at Amal's school?

Why did Theo trip and fall in the relay race?

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq015 · unit 3 · Short answer · The Big Race

What kind thing did Amal do after she won, and why do you think she did it?

answer: She walked back and helped Theo stand up. Any reason that uses the story, such as Theo looking sad or rubbing his hurt knee.

Amal saw Theo sitting on the grass and put out her hand instead of celebrating. A good answer gives evidence from the story in the reason.

the text it is asked about

Next came the big relay race. In a relay, the runners take turns. Each runner passes a stick to the next one. Amal was the last runner for the green team.
“Ready, steady, go!” called Teacher Yasmin again.
Nora ran fast. Sami ran faster. Then Leo passed the stick to Amal. But Amal's tummy felt full of butterflies. Her legs felt slow.
“I am running as fast as I can,” Amal puffed. “But Theo is winning!”
Theo was far in front. He was busy waving to the crowd. He did not look at his feet. Suddenly he tripped and fell onto the grass. His stick rolled away.
Amal ran past him. Her team was cheering. “Go, Amal, go!” they shouted, clapping and jumping. Amal reached the line first. The green team had won!
Everyone cheered and waved. But Amal looked back. Theo was still sitting on the grass. He was rubbing his knee, and he looked very sad.
Amal did not jump for joy. She walked back to Theo and put out her hand.
“Are you hurt?” she asked kindly.
“Only a little,” said Theo. “But I lost. And I said so many silly things.”
Amal smiled. “You are still the fastest runner in the school,” she said. “Come on. Stand up.”
She helped Theo stand. They walked back together, and all the children clapped for them.
Teacher Yasmin nodded and smiled. “Well done, everyone,” she said. “Amal, you ran your best and you never gave up. And you were kind to Theo. That is the true winner.”
That evening, Amal told her mother about the big race. “I won,” she said, “but the best part was helping Theo. Next time, we will be on the same team!”
Her mother hugged her. “You have a fast body and a kind heart,” she said. “Now drink your water and rest. Tomorrow is another day to move and play.”

eng-g02-t01-u03-cq016 · unit 3 · Short answer · The Big Race

How did Amal feel when her legs felt slow, and what did she do instead of giving up?

answer: Her tummy felt full of butterflies and she felt worried, but she kept running as fast as she could.

The butterflies in her tummy show her nerves, and her own words show that she carried on running.

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq001 · unit 4 · Short answer · A Poem: “My Shadow”

The poet writes about his shadow as if it were a person. Which little word does he use instead of “it”?

answer: Him.

The poem says “what can be the use of him”, so the shadow is called him, not it.

the text it is asked about
Read this poem slowly, then say it out loud:
I have a little shadow that goes in and out with me,
And what can be the use of him is more than I can see.
He is very, very like me from the heels up to the head,
And I see him jump before me, when I jump into my bed.
(by Robert Louis Stevenson)

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq002 · unit 4 · Short answer · A Poem: “My Shadow”

Where does the little shadow go with the poet?

answer: It goes in and out with him everywhere, and it even goes with him into bed.

Line one says the shadow goes in and out with him, and the last line takes it right into his bed.

the text it is asked about

(by Robert Louis Stevenson)

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq003 · unit 4 · Short answer · A Poem: “My Shadow”

The poem says the shadow is “very, very like me”. From where to where is it like the poet?

answer: From the heels up to the head, which is his whole body.

Line three gives the two ends: the heels at the bottom and the head at the top.

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq004 · unit 4 · Short answer · A Poem: “My Shadow”

In the last line, what does the shadow do at the very moment the poet gets into bed?

answer: It jumps before him, so it seems to jump into bed first.

The poem ends with the shadow jumping ahead of the poet. A good answer gives the jump and says when it happens.

(by Robert Louis Stevenson)

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq005 · unit 4 · Short answer · Telling Time with a Shadow

What does a sundial use to tell the time?

answer: A shadow.

The text says a clock uses hands, but a sundial uses a shadow that moves as the sun moves.

the text it is asked about
Read this short text:
Amal found a strange stone in Grandma Hana’s garden. It had numbers carved around the edge and a thin metal triangle standing straight up in the middle.
“What is this stone, Grandma?” asked Amal.
“That is my sundial,” said Grandma Hana. “It is like a clock, but it has no hands.”
“No hands? Then how does it tell the time?” asked Amal.
“Watch the metal triangle,” said Grandma Hana. “The sun shines on it and makes a shadow. The shadow points at a number, and that number is the hour.”
Amal’s brother, Adam, came to look too. “So the shadow is like the hand on a clock!” he said.
“Exactly,” said Grandma Hana. “A clock uses hands that point at numbers. A sundial uses a moving shadow instead, and it only works when the sun is shining.”
Early that morning, the three of them checked the sundial. The sun was still low, and the shadow stretched long across the stone, all the way past the number seven.
“The shadow is so long,” said Amal.
“Yes,” said Grandma Hana. “When the sun is low, the shadow is long. Come back at midday and see what happens.”
At midday, Amal ran straight to the garden. The sun was high overhead now, and the shadow had shrunk. It was short, barely reaching the first number.
“It is tiny!” laughed Amal. “Where did the long shadow go?”
“When the sun is high, the shadow is short,” explained Adam. “Remember how your own shadow does the same thing?”
In the evening, they visited the sundial one last time. The sun was sinking low again, and the shadow had grown long, stretching out the other way across the stone.
“Long in the morning, short at midday, long again in the evening,” said Amal, counting on her fingers.
“You have learned it well,” said Grandma Hana. “Long before clocks were invented, people all over the world used sundials just like this one to tell the time. They watched the sun and the shadow, and they knew when to work, when to eat, and when to rest.”
Amal traced the shadow with her finger. “I love this stone clock,” she said. “The sun tells the time, and the shadow shows us!”

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq006 · unit 4 · Short answer · Telling Time with a Shadow

When is the shadow on a sundial short, and when is it long?

answer: It is short at midday, and it is long in the morning and long again in the evening.

Three sentences in the middle of the text give the three times of day.

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq007 · unit 4 · Short answer · Telling Time with a Shadow

How is a sundial the same as a clock, and how is it different?

answer: Both tell the time. A clock uses hands that point at numbers, while a sundial uses a moving shadow and needs sunlight.

Grandma Hana gives the likeness when she says a sundial “is like a clock”, and the difference when she says a clock uses hands but a sundial uses a moving shadow and only works in sunshine. A good answer gives both halves.

the text it is asked about

Amal found a strange stone in Grandma Hana’s garden. It had numbers carved around the edge and a thin metal triangle standing straight up in the middle.

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq008 · unit 4 · Short answer · Nora's Shadow

When is Nora's shadow long, and when is it short?

answer: It is long in the morning and in the evening, and it is short at midday.

Nora describes her shadow three times, once for each part of the day.

the text it is asked about
Listen to Nora. (Ask an adult or your AI tutor to read it aloud if one is nearby, or read it aloud yourself.)
“My name is Nora, and I want to tell you about my shadow. Every day it changes, and I have watched it happen again and again.
In the morning, I walk to school with my friend Amal. The sun is still low in the sky, just peeking over the rooftops. When I look down, my shadow stretches out in front of me, long and thin, like a giant version of myself walking ahead. One morning Amal laughed and said my shadow was taller than me. I told her that was because the sun was low, and that Adam had taught me: when the sun is low, shadows are long.
By midday, everything looks different. We sit outside for lunch, and the sun is high above our heads, almost straight up. I look down at my feet, and my shadow has almost disappeared. It is short and small, hiding right underneath me like a tiny dark puddle. Amal asked where my long shadow had gone. I told her the sun was high now, and when the sun is high, shadows get short.
In the evening, when school finishes and I walk home, the sun sinks low again on the other side of the sky. My shadow grows long once more, stretching out behind me this time instead of in front. It follows me all the way to my front door.
I used to think my shadow just did whatever it wanted. Now I know better. My shadow always follows one simple rule: it copies the sun. When the sun is low, in the morning and in the evening, my shadow is long. When the sun is high, at midday, my shadow is short.
Try it yourself tomorrow. Look at your shadow three times: once in the morning, once at midday, and once in the evening. Ask yourself where the sun is each time, and see if your shadow follows the same rule as mine.”

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq009 · unit 4 · Short answer · Nora's Shadow

Why does Nora's shadow change during the day? Use the word sun in your answer.

answer: Because the sun is low in the morning and in the evening, and high at midday.

Nora links every change to where the sun is, so the answer must name the sun.

the text it is asked about

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq010 · unit 4 · Short answer · Why We Have Day and Night

According to the text, what makes it day where you live, and what makes it night?

answer: It is day when your part of Earth faces the sun, and night when your part turns away from the sun.

The text gives both halves in two sentences that begin with the word when.

the text it is asked about
Listen and follow along. (Ask an adult or your AI tutor to read it aloud if one is nearby, or read it aloud yourself.)
One evening, Amal sat on the mat with her brother, Adam, and watched the sky turn orange.
“Adam,” said Amal, “why does the sun disappear every night? Does it go to sleep?”
“The sun does not disappear, and it never sleeps,” said Adam. “Let me show you something.” He fetched a torch and an orange ball from Amal’s room.
“This torch is the sun,” said Adam, switching it on. “And this ball is our planet, Earth. The sun shines on Earth all the time, but Earth is always turning, round and round, very slowly. It takes a whole day and a whole night for Earth to turn all the way around.”
He held the torch still and turned the ball slowly in front of it. “Watch this spot,” he said, pointing to a small mark he had drawn on the ball. “When the mark faces the torch, that part of Earth has light. That is day.”
Amal watched the mark turn towards the light. “It is bright now!” she said.
“Keep watching,” said Adam, turning the ball further. Slowly the mark turned away from the torch, into the shadow of the ball itself. “Now the mark faces away from the sun. No sunlight reaches it. That is night.”
“So day and night happen because Earth is spinning, not because the sun moves?” asked Amal.
“Exactly,” said Adam. “The sun stays still, in a way. It is Earth that turns. When your home faces the sun, you have day. When your home turns away from the sun, you have night.”
Amal thought about the sunrise she saw every morning. “But the sun looks like it is rising over the hills,” she said. “Doesn’t it move?”
“It only seems that way,” said Adam. “As Earth slowly turns your part of the world towards the sun, the sun seems to rise. As Earth turns your part away, the sun seems to set. The sun stays in its place. We are the ones moving.”
Amal looked up at the darkening sky, where the first stars were beginning to appear. “So right now,” she said slowly, “our part of Earth is turning away from the sun.”
“That’s it,” said Adam. “Night is really just Earth turning us into the shadow, ready to turn us back towards morning.”
Amal smiled and looked at the little ball still spinning gently in Adam’s hands, carrying her slowly towards tomorrow.

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq011 · unit 4 · Short answer · Why We Have Day and Night

The text says the sun “seems to rise”. Does the sun really travel across the sky, or is something else moving?

answer: The sun does not travel. Earth turns slowly, so the sun only seems to rise and set.

Adam says it is Earth that turns, and then explains that as Earth turns our part of the world towards the sun and away from it, the sun only seems to rise and set.

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq012 · unit 4 · Short answer · The Night Amal Counted the Stars

What colour was the sky when Amal woke up in the morning?

answer: It was pale and pink.

The second sentence of the story describes the morning sky before the sun has climbed.

the text it is asked about
In a small town near the sea, Amal woke up early. The morning sky was pale and pink. The sun was low. It climbed slowly into the big sky.
Amal ran outside to feed the goats. On the sandy ground she saw a long, thin shape. It moved when she moved.
“Look, Adam!” she called to her big brother. “My shadow is so long this morning!”
Adam smiled. “In the morning the sun is low,” he said. “When the sun is low, your shadow is long. Watch what happens at midday.”
By midday the sun was high and bright. Amal stood very still and looked down. Her shadow was now short. It hid right under her feet!
“It shrank!” she laughed. “Where did my long shadow go?”
“The sun is high, so your shadow is short,” said Adam. “The sun did not move by itself. Our Earth is turning, round and round, all day long.”
Amal thought about that. The whole Earth was turning, but she could not feel it. She looked up. A few white clouds floated across the blue sky. One cloud drifted over the sun. Her shadow disappeared.
“The cloud blocked the light!” said Amal. “No light, no shadow.”
“Now you understand shadows,” said Adam.
In the evening, the sky turned orange and red. The sun was low again, and Amal's shadow grew long. Their grandmother, Grandma Hana, sat outside on a mat.
“Come and sit with me, Amal,” she said softly. “The day is leaving, and the night is coming.”
Slowly the sky grew dark. One by one, tiny lights began to shine. Amal had never really looked before.
“Grandma,” she whispered, “there are so many stars!”
“More than anyone can count,” said Grandma Hana. “Each star is like our sun, but very, very far away. Our sun is only a star that is close to us.”
Amal tried to count anyway. “One, two, three, four…” But every time she looked, she found more. She reached fifty and gave up, laughing.
Then the moon rose over the hills, round and bright.
“Why does the moon shine, Grandma?” Amal asked. “Does it have its own light, like the sun?”
“No, little one,” said her grandmother. “The moon has no light of its own. The sun shines on it. The moon gives us that light at night. Is it not beautiful?”
Amal nodded. She thought about the whole big sky. She thought about the sun that changed her shadow. She thought about the clouds and the countless stars. She thought about the gentle moon.
“Grandma,” she said, “the sky is full of wonderful things. I never really looked up before.”
Grandma Hana smiled and held her hand. “Curious people wonder about the world,” she said. “They look. They ask questions. They say thank you for this beauty. Tomorrow the sun will rise, and a new day will begin.”
That night Amal fell asleep happy. In her dreams the stars were still shining. She counted them one by one across the big sky.

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq013 · unit 4 · Short answer · The Night Amal Counted the Stars

Why was Amal's shadow long in the morning but short at midday?

answer: In the morning the sun was low, so her shadow was long. At midday the sun was high, so her shadow was short.

Adam explains the rule twice, once for the morning and once for midday.

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq014 · unit 4 · Short answer · The Night Amal Counted the Stars

What happened to Amal's shadow when a cloud drifted over the sun, and why?

answer: It disappeared for a moment, because the cloud blocked the light.

Amal says it herself: “No light, no shadow.” The cloud stopped the sunlight reaching her.

eng-g02-t02-u04-cq016 · unit 4 · Short answer · The Night Amal Counted the Stars

Why does the moon shine, according to Grandma Hana?

answer: The moon has no light of its own. The sun shines on it, and the moon gives that light back to us at night.

Grandma Hana answers Amal's question in the paragraph just after the moon rises.

the text it is asked about

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq001 · unit 5 · Short answer · Shapes All Around Us

What shape is the sun in this text, and what shape is the door?

answer: The sun is a circle and the door is a rectangle.

Amal says the sun is a circle, and Leo says the door is a rectangle because two of its sides are longer than the other two.

the text it is asked about
Read this story about a shape hunt at school.

On Monday morning, Teacher Yasmin held up a big paper circle. “Shapes are all around us,” she said. “Today we are going on a shape hunt around the classroom and the yard. Find one example of every shape, and be ready to describe it.”

Amal looked up first. “The clock on the wall is a circle,” she said, “and so is the sun outside the window.”

Leo pointed at the door. “That window beside the door is a square,” he said, “because all four sides are the same length.” He looked again. “But the door itself is a rectangle. Two of its sides are longer than the other two.”

Nora crouched down by the bookshelf. “Look at the roof on our model house,” she said. “It is a triangle. It has three straight sides and three corners.”

“Well done, all of you,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Now, who can find a heart?”

Theo lifted a small red cushion from the reading corner. “Here is a heart,” he said, tracing its curved top and pointed bottom with his finger. “It does not look like the other shapes at all.”

Teacher Yasmin smiled. “Excellent. Now let's make a pattern using what we found. Circle, square, triangle, circle, square, triangle — can anyone tell me what comes next?”

“Another circle!” called Maya from the back of the room. “Because the pattern always repeats in the same order.”

“Exactly right,” said Teacher Yasmin. “A pattern is a group of shapes, colours or numbers that repeat in the same order, again and again.”

Before lunch, the class walked to the market square outside the school gate. Amal counted rectangle windows on the tall building. Leo found round wheels on a passing cart. Nora spotted triangle flags strung above a fruit stall, and Theo noticed a heart drawn in chalk on the pavement.

“Shapes are everywhere once you start looking,” said Teacher Yasmin, as the children walked back through the gate. “Tonight, look around your own home. How many shapes can you find before bedtime?”

That evening, Amal found six shapes in her own kitchen — and she could not wait to tell her class all about it in the morning.

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq002 · unit 5 · Short answer · Shapes All Around Us

The text gives one shape pattern as an example. Write that pattern in the right order.

answer: Circle, square, triangle, circle, square, triangle.

The pattern is given near the end of the text, and the three shapes repeat in exactly the same order.

Theo lifted a small red cushion from the reading corner. “Here is a heart,” he said, tracing its curved top and pointed bottom with his finger. “It does not look like the other shapes at all.”

Teacher Yasmin smiled. “Excellent. Now let's make a pattern using what we found. Circle, square, triangle, circle, square, triangle — can anyone tell me what comes next?”

“Another circle!” called Maya from the back of the room. “Because the pattern always repeats in the same order.”

“Exactly right,” said Teacher Yasmin. “A pattern is a group of shapes, colours or numbers that repeat in the same order, again and again.”

Before lunch, the class walked to the market square outside the school gate. Amal counted rectangle windows on the tall building. Leo found round wheels on a passing cart. Nora spotted triangle flags strung above a fruit stall, and Theo noticed a heart drawn in chalk on the pavement.

“Shapes are everywhere once you start looking,” said Teacher Yasmin, as the children walked back through the gate. “Tonight, look around your own home. How many shapes can you find before bedtime?”

That evening, Amal found six shapes in her own kitchen — and she could not wait to tell her class all about it in the morning.

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq004 · unit 5 · Short answer · A Counting Song

What is being counted in the counting song?

answer: Little fingers.

Every line of the song names fingers, so fingers are what the singer is counting.

the text it is asked about
Read and sing this song. Count in tens as you go:
10 little, 20 little, 30 little fingers. / 40 little, 50 little, 60 little fingers. / 70 little, 80 little, 90 little fingers. / 100 little fingers in the air!

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq005 · unit 5 · Short answer · A Counting Song

How many fingers are in the air at the end of the song?

answer: There are one hundred fingers in the air.

The last line of the song reaches one hundred, which is the biggest number in the whole song.

the text it is asked about
Read and sing this song. Count in tens as you go:
10 little, 20 little, 30 little fingers. / 40 little, 50 little, 60 little fingers. / 70 little, 80 little, 90 little fingers. / 100 little fingers in the air!

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq006 · unit 5 · Short answer · A Counting Song

Each child has ten fingers. How many children would you need to show one hundred fingers?

answer: Ten children.

One hundred is ten groups of ten, so ten children each holding up ten fingers make one hundred.

the text it is asked about
Read and sing this song. Count in tens as you go:
10 little, 20 little, 30 little fingers. / 40 little, 50 little, 60 little fingers. / 70 little, 80 little, 90 little fingers. / 100 little fingers in the air!

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq007 · unit 5 · Short answer · Using Numbers in Class

How is Nora using numbers, and how many shapes does she count altogether?

answer: She is counting shapes on a chart, and she counts fifty shapes altogether.

The text says Nora counts 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, so her final total is fifty.

the text it is asked about
Listen to this story about children using numbers, then read it back yourself.

It was Tuesday afternoon, and Class 2 was busy with number activities. Teacher Yasmin walked between the tables, watching every group work.

At the first table, Nora was counting shapes on a big chart. She touched each row and counted out loud: “Ten, twenty, thirty, forty, fifty.” She looked up, pleased with herself. “There are fifty shapes altogether, Teacher Yasmin!”

“Well counted,” said Teacher Yasmin. “You counted carefully in tens, and that made fifty shapes much faster to count than counting one by one.”

Near the window, Leo was measuring how far he could jump. He placed a metre stick on the classroom floor, jumped from the starting line, and asked Amal to read the number.

“You jumped forty-five centimetres, Leo!” said Amal, kneeling beside the metre stick.

“Let me try again,” said Leo. “I think I can jump further this time.” He jumped once more, and this time the metre stick showed fifty centimetres.

“You beat your own number!” laughed Amal. “Fifty is more than forty-five.”

Across the room, Amal herself was looking at the classroom clock. The big hand pointed to the six, and the small hand sat between the two and the three.

“It is half past two,” said Amal to herself. “That means we have thirty more minutes until home time.”

Teacher Yasmin clapped her hands gently to bring the class together. “Today you used numbers in three different ways,” she said. “Nora counted in tens to find a total. Leo measured a distance in centimetres. And Amal read the time on the clock. Numbers help us count things, measure things, and tell the time.”

“I like numbers,” said Nora. “They help me understand the whole world around me.”

“So do I,” said Leo, rubbing his knee after his big jump. “Especially when they tell me I jumped further than before!”

The whole class laughed, and Teacher Yasmin wrote three words on the board: count, measure, time. “Remember these three jobs that numbers do,” she said, “and you will use numbers well for the rest of your life.”

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq008 · unit 5 · Short answer · Using Numbers in Class

How far did Leo jump the first time, and how far the second time?

The text says Leo is measuring how far he can jump, and gives the exact number in centimetres.

answer: Numbers help us count, measure and tell the time.

Each of the three children shows one of the three uses, and the text lists them together.

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq020 · unit 5 · Short answer · How People Measured Long Ago

Is ‘How People Measured Long Ago’ about today or long ago, and which places does it visit?

answer: Long ago, in Egypt and in Rome.

Teacher Yasmin tells a story about “long, long ago - before rulers and metre sticks were invented”, first in Egypt and then in Rome. Stories can come from other times and other places.

the text it is asked about
Listen to this story about measuring long ago, then think about the questions below.

“Before we start today's measuring lesson,” said Teacher Yasmin, “let me tell you a story about how people measured things long, long ago — before rulers and metre sticks were invented.”

The class gathered on the mat, and Teacher Yasmin began.

“Long ago in Egypt, people did not have rulers,” she said. “Instead, they measured with their own bodies. They used the width of a finger for very small things, the length of a hand for medium things, and the length of an arm for bigger things, like a piece of cloth.”

“But wouldn't that give different answers for different people?” asked Amal, remembering how her feet and Leo's feet had once caused an argument.

“You are exactly right, Amal,” said Teacher Yasmin. “A big person's arm is longer than a small person's arm, so their measurements did not always match.”

“What about other countries?” asked Leo.

“Long ago in Rome,” Teacher Yasmin continued, “people often measured distances by counting their footsteps. A messenger might say a market was 'one thousand footsteps away.' But a tall soldier's footstep covered more ground than a child's footstep, so the same distance could be counted differently by different people.”

Nora raised her hand. “So how did people finally solve the problem?”

“Slowly, over hundreds of years, people agreed to use the same tools everywhere,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Today we use a ruler for small things and a metre stick for longer things, because a centimetre and a metre are exactly the same length for everyone, in every country, every single time.”

“That sounds much fairer,” said Amal.

“It is,” said Teacher Yasmin, smiling. “A fair measurement is the same for everyone — no matter how big or small your hands and feet happen to be.”

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq010 · unit 5 · Short answer · How People Measured Long Ago

What did people use to measure long ago in Egypt, and what did they use in Rome?

answer: In Egypt they measured with fingers, hands and arms. In Rome they measured in footsteps.

The text names one method for each place, so a full answer keeps the two apart.

the text it is asked about

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq011 · unit 5 · Short answer · How People Measured Long Ago

Why does the text say a ruler is better than measuring with our own feet?

answer: Because a ruler and a metre stick are the same for everyone, but feet are all different sizes.

The last sentence gives the reason: the tool is the same for everyone, so the answer never changes.

the text it is asked about

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq012 · unit 5 · Short answer · A Fair Way to Measure

What did the children in the story want to make, and how long did it need to be?

answer: A new reading corner, with a mat long enough for six friends to sit on.

Teacher Yasmin explains the plan in her first speech, including how many friends the mat must hold.

the text it is asked about
Beside the busy town market there was a small school with a shady tree in the yard. One morning, Teacher Yasmin had good news.
“Children, we are going to make a new reading corner,” she said. “We need a mat that is long enough for six friends to sit on. Who can measure the space for me?”
Amal jumped up. “I can measure with my feet!” she said. She walked heel to toe across the floor. “One, two, three … the space is fourteen feet long!”
Leo laughed. “Let me try too.” He walked across the same floor. “One, two, three … I count only eleven feet. You are wrong, Amal!”
“No, you are wrong!” said Amal. The two friends began to argue.
Nora looked down at their feet. “Wait,” she said kindly. “Look. Leo's feet are big. Amal's feet are small. That is why your answers are different. Feet are not a fair way to measure.”
Amal stopped. “You are right, Nora,” she said quietly. “We need one measure that is the same for everyone.”
Teacher Yasmin smiled. “Well done, Nora. Long ago, people measured with their hands and feet. But hands and feet are all different sizes. So today we use a ruler and a metre stick. A metre is always the same length.”
She gave them a long metre stick. “There are one hundred centimetres in one metre,” she said. “Now measure carefully, and be patient.”
The children measured the floor together, slowly and carefully. It was three metres long. Amal wrote the number down. This time, nobody argued.
While they worked, Sami carried two baskets of cushions. “This basket is heavy,” he said, lifting the big one. “And this one is light.” Maya ran to help him carry the heavy basket, so the load was fair for both of them.
Daniel held up two ribbons to tie the cushions. “This red ribbon is longer,” he said. “This green ribbon is shorter. We need the longer one for the big cushion.”
Soon the mat was ready. It looked wonderful. Everyone wanted to sit down first.
“I am the tallest, so I should sit first!” said Theo, standing up straight.
“That is not fair,” said Amal gently. “Being tall does not mean you go first. Let us all sit together.”
Theo thought for a moment. “You are right,” he said. “I am sorry.” He made room, and everyone sat down side by side.
Teacher Yasmin clapped her hands. “Today you learned three important things,” she said. “You learned to measure carefully. You learned to be patient. And you learned to be fair. A fair measure is the same for everyone — and so is a kind heart.”
Amal looked at Leo and smiled. “Your feet are bigger than mine,” she said, “but our mat is the same for both of us.”
Leo laughed. “And it is long enough for all six of us!”
The friends sat on their new mat under the shady tree and opened their books. Outside, the market was noisy and busy. But in the little reading corner, everything felt just right.

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq013 · unit 5 · Short answer · A Fair Way to Measure

Why did Amal and Leo get different answers when they measured the floor with their feet?

answer: Leo's feet are big and Amal's feet are small, so the same floor took a different number of feet.

Nora spots the reason by looking down at their feet, and explains it to both of them.
