Theo thought for a moment. “You are right,” he said. “I am sorry.” He made room, and everyone sat down side by side.
Teacher Yasmin clapped her hands. “Today you learned three important things,” she said. “You learned to measure carefully. You learned to be patient. And you learned to be fair. A fair measure is the same for everyone — and so is a kind heart.”
Amal looked at Leo and smiled. “Your feet are bigger than mine,” she said, “but our mat is the same for both of us.”
Leo laughed. “And it is long enough for all six of us!”
The friends sat on their new mat under the shady tree and opened their books. Outside, the market was noisy and busy. But in the little reading corner, everything felt just right.

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq017 · unit 5 · Short answer · A Fair Way to Measure

Why did Amal say it was not fair for Theo to sit down first, and what did Theo do next?

answer: Being tall does not mean you go first. Theo thought about it, said sorry and made room for everyone.

Amal answers Theo gently rather than crossly, and Theo changes his mind straight afterwards.

the text it is asked about
Beside the busy town market there was a small school with a shady tree in the yard. One morning, Teacher Yasmin had good news.
“Children, we are going to make a new reading corner,” she said. “We need a mat that is long enough for six friends to sit on. Who can measure the space for me?”
Amal jumped up. “I can measure with my feet!” she said. She walked heel to toe across the floor. “One, two, three … the space is fourteen feet long!”
Leo laughed. “Let me try too.” He walked across the same floor. “One, two, three … I count only eleven feet. You are wrong, Amal!”
“No, you are wrong!” said Amal. The two friends began to argue.
Nora looked down at their feet. “Wait,” she said kindly. “Look. Leo's feet are big. Amal's feet are small. That is why your answers are different. Feet are not a fair way to measure.”
Amal stopped. “You are right, Nora,” she said quietly. “We need one measure that is the same for everyone.”
Teacher Yasmin smiled. “Well done, Nora. Long ago, people measured with their hands and feet. But hands and feet are all different sizes. So today we use a ruler and a metre stick. A metre is always the same length.”
She gave them a long metre stick. “There are one hundred centimetres in one metre,” she said. “Now measure carefully, and be patient.”
The children measured the floor together, slowly and carefully. It was three metres long. Amal wrote the number down. This time, nobody argued.
While they worked, Sami carried two baskets of cushions. “This basket is heavy,” he said, lifting the big one. “And this one is light.” Maya ran to help him carry the heavy basket, so the load was fair for both of them.
Daniel held up two ribbons to tie the cushions. “This red ribbon is longer,” he said. “This green ribbon is shorter. We need the longer one for the big cushion.”
Soon the mat was ready. It looked wonderful. Everyone wanted to sit down first.
“I am the tallest, so I should sit first!” said Theo, standing up straight.
“That is not fair,” said Amal gently. “Being tall does not mean you go first. Let us all sit together.”
Theo thought for a moment. “You are right,” he said. “I am sorry.” He made room, and everyone sat down side by side.
Teacher Yasmin clapped her hands. “Today you learned three important things,” she said. “You learned to measure carefully. You learned to be patient. And you learned to be fair. A fair measure is the same for everyone — and so is a kind heart.”
Amal looked at Leo and smiled. “Your feet are bigger than mine,” she said, “but our mat is the same for both of us.”
Leo laughed. “And it is long enough for all six of us!”
The friends sat on their new mat under the shady tree and opened their books. Outside, the market was noisy and busy. But in the little reading corner, everything felt just right.

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq018 · unit 5 · Short answer · A Fair Way to Measure

Teacher Yasmin said, “A fair measure is the same for everyone.” What do you think she meant?

answer: That a good measure, such as a metre, never changes from person to person, so nobody is treated unfairly.

Link the answer back to the feet at the start of the story, which changed with every child.

the text it is asked about

eng-g02-t02-u05-cq019 · unit 5 · Short answer · A Fair Way to Measure

The children measured the floor slowly and carefully. Tell about a time when you had to be patient like that. What happened?

answer: Any personal example that names the task, says what you did to stay patient, and says how it ended.

This question links the story to your own life, so compare your patience with the children's.

the text it is asked about

eng-g02-t02-u06-cq001 · unit 6 · Short answer · What Is an Insect?

How many legs do all insects have, and what are their three main body parts called?

answer: Six legs. The three main body parts are the head, the thorax and the abdomen.

The text gives both answers, one after the other.

the text it is asked about
Read this short text:
Leo found a beetle walking across the classroom windowsill, and he ran to ask Teacher Yasmin about it.
“Teacher Yasmin, is a beetle an insect?” asked Leo.
“Let's find out together,” said Teacher Yasmin. “First, count its legs. How many do you see?”
Leo counted carefully. “One, two, three... six! It has six legs.”
“That is the first clue,” said Teacher Yasmin. “All insects have six legs. Now look at its body. Can you see three parts?”
Leo looked closely. “Yes! There is a head, then a middle part, and then a longer part at the back.”
“Good,” said Teacher Yasmin. “The three body parts are called the head, the thorax, and the abdomen. The thorax is the middle part, where the legs and wings join on. The abdomen is the back part.”
“What are those two thin bits on its head?” asked Leo.
“Those are antennae,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Antennae help an insect feel and smell things around it, almost like a nose and fingers all in one. Most insects have two antennae.”
“Does every insect have wings?” asked Leo.
“Many insects have wings, but not all of them,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Butterflies, bees and crickets all have wings and can fly. But some insects, like certain kinds of ants, have no wings at all.”
Just then, Nora came over holding a jar. “I found a spider in the garden. Is it an insect too?”
Teacher Yasmin smiled. “Count its legs, Nora.”
Nora counted. “One, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight! It has eight legs.”
“Then it is not an insect,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Insects have six legs, but a spider has eight legs. That is why we say a spider is not an insect.”
“What about a worm?” asked Leo. “I found one under a rock yesterday.”
“A worm has no legs at all,” said Teacher Yasmin, “so it is not an insect either. Insects, spiders and worms are all small creatures, but only insects have six legs, three body parts and antennae.”
Leo looked at the beetle again. “So this really is an insect!”
“It is,” said Teacher Yasmin. “And do you know something amazing? There are more kinds of insects in the world than any other type of animal. Millions and millions of them live all around us.”
Leo grinned and gently placed the beetle back on the windowsill, where it crawled away into the sunshine.

eng-g02-t02-u06-cq002 · unit 6 · Short answer · What Is an Insect?

According to the text, what do antennae help insects do?

answer: They help insects feel and smell the things around them.

The text says most insects have two antennae on their heads, and then explains what those antennae are for.

The text says a spider and a worm are not insects. Give the reason for each one.

Teacher Yasmin gives each reason: a spider has eight legs and a worm has no legs at all, but insects have six legs.

eng-g02-t02-u06-cq004 · unit 6 · Short answer · A Bug Poem

In the poem, where is the bug at the start?

answer: The bug is on the speaker, because the poem says, “There's a bug on me.”

Line two of the poem tells you exactly where the bug is sitting.

the text it is asked about
Read this short poem, then try to say it from memory:
One, two, three!
There's a bug on me.
Where did it go?
I don't know.

eng-g02-t02-u06-cq005 · unit 6 · Short answer · A Bug Poem

The poem asks, “Where did it go?” What answer does the very next line give?

answer: “I don't know.” Nobody in the poem can say where the bug went.

The poem is a short question and answer. Reading the last two lines together gives the reply.

the text it is asked about

One, two, three!
There's a bug on me.
Where did it go?
I don't know.

eng-g02-t02-u06-cq006 · unit 6 · Short answer · A Bug Poem

Find one pair of words in the poem that rhyme with each other.

answer: “Three” and “me” rhyme, and “go” and “know” also rhyme.

Poems often put rhyming words at the ends of lines. Say the last word of each line aloud to hear the pairs.

I don't know.

eng-g02-t02-u06-cq007 · unit 6 · Short answer · Grandpa’s Cricket

What was in Grandpa's pocket, and where had he found it?

answer: A cricket, and he found it under a stone in the garden.

Grandpa answers Nora's first question with both the creature and the place he found it.

the text it is asked about
Listen to Nora talk with her grandpa. Ask an adult to read it aloud if one is nearby, or read it aloud yourself.
Nora was helping Grandpa water the tomato plants when she noticed something moving inside his shirt pocket.
“Grandpa, what is that in your pocket?” asked Nora.
Grandpa reached in carefully and opened his hand. A small brown cricket sat on his palm, its long back legs folded neatly beneath it.
“It is a cricket,” he said. “I found it under a stone in the garden while I was digging.”
Nora leaned in close. The cricket rubbed its wings together and made a soft, clicking chirp.
“Can I keep it as a pet? I love the chirping sound!” said Nora.
Grandpa smiled and sat down on the garden bench. “A cricket can make a fine pet,” he said, “but if you keep a cricket, you must take good care of it. Are you ready to learn how?”
“Yes!” said Nora. “Tell me everything.”
“First,” said Grandpa, “crickets need to stay warm. They do not like cold weather, so keep the jar somewhere warm, away from open windows.”
Nora nodded and looked at the little cricket. “What does it eat?”
“Crickets eat small pieces of fruit and vegetables,” Grandpa explained. “A slice of apple or a piece of lettuce will make a happy cricket.”
“And what about water?” asked Nora.
“They also need a little dish of water,” said Grandpa, “but only a very small, shallow dish. A cricket cannot swim, so deep water is dangerous for it.”
Nora ran inside and came back with an old glass jar with holes poked in the lid, a tiny slice of apple, and a bottle cap filled with water.
“Very good,” said Grandpa. “Now put in some soft grass too, so your cricket has somewhere to hide.”
Nora gently placed the cricket inside the jar. It hopped once, then settled into the grass and began to chirp again.
“Listen,” whispered Nora. “I think he likes his new home.”
“He does sound happy,” said Grandpa, laughing. “Now remember, Nora — a pet cricket does not live forever, so after a few days, you might let him go back to the garden, where he can chirp for the whole family to hear at night.”
Nora thought about this. “I will take care of him first, and then I will let him go home,” she said. “Thank you, Grandpa.”
“That is exactly right,” said Grandpa. “Taking care of a small creature, even for a short time, teaches us to be gentle and patient.”
That night, Nora fell asleep listening to her cricket's steady chirp through her open bedroom door, the happiest sound in the whole house.

eng-g02-t02-u06-cq008 · unit 6 · Short answer · Grandpa’s Cricket

eng-g02-t02-u06-cq010 · unit 6 · Short answer · Amal and the Little Garden Friends

At the start of the story, what did Amal do when a bee buzzed near her, and what did she do when she saw a spider?

answer: She ran away from the bee, and she covered her eyes when she saw the spider.

The second paragraph gives both reactions in two short sentences, one for each creature.

the text it is asked about
Early one warm morning, Amal went out to the garden behind her house. The sun was soft and gold. Birds sang from the tall tree by the wall. The air smelled of wet soil after the rain.
Amal loved the garden. But there was one thing she did not love: bugs. When a bee buzzed near her, she ran. When she saw a spider, she covered her eyes.
Her older brother Adam was already there. He sat very still on a low rock.
“Come, Amal,” he whispered. “Sit with me. If you look closely, the garden is full of little friends.”
Amal sat down slowly. On a bright red flower, a butterfly opened and closed its wings.
“Look,” said Adam. “A butterfly is an insect. It has six legs, and two antennae on its head. Its antennae help it feel and smell. It drinks sweet nectar from the flower.”
The butterfly did not seem scary at all. Amal smiled a little.
Then a bee flew above the flowers, going from one to the next.
“Is the bee going to sting me?” Amal asked, pulling back.
“No,” said Adam gently. “The bee is very busy. It is collecting nectar to make honey. Stay calm and do not wave your arms. Then the bee will leave you alone.”
Amal held still. The bee buzzed near her hand, then flew away. She let out a happy breath. She was not afraid now.
Near her foot, a long line of ants marched over the soil. They were carrying tiny seeds.
“Where are they going?” Amal asked.
“To their home,” said Adam. “Ants live in a big anthill under the ground. They work together and share everything. One ant is small, but many ants are very strong.”
Amal watched the ants for a long time. She liked how they helped each other, just like a big family.
Then she saw something between two branches of the small lemon tree. It was a web, shining with drops of water. In the middle sat a spider.
Amal wanted to run, but she did not.
“A spider is not an insect,” said Adam. “Count its legs.”
Amal counted, one by one. “Eight legs!” she said. “Insects have only six.”
“That is right,” said Adam, proud of her. “A spider spins its web from silk in its own body. The web catches insects for the spider to eat. It is not here to hurt you. It is only doing its work.”
Amal looked at the web. It was beautiful, like tiny threads of glass.
Just then, their little sister Mina came running. She lifted her foot to stamp on a fat worm on the path.
“Wait!” called Amal. “Do not step on it. A worm has no legs, but it is a good little animal. Worms crawl through the soil and help our plants grow strong.”
Mina stopped. Together, the three children watched the worm wriggle safely under a leaf.
That evening, Amal told her mother everything. “I used to be afraid of bugs,” she said. “But now I know they are little friends. The bee makes honey and the ants work together. The spider spins a web and the worm helps the soil.”
Her mother smiled. “When we understand something,” she said, “we stop being afraid of it. And when we care for even the smallest creature, our hearts grow bigger.”
Amal looked out at the dark garden. All her little friends were resting. She could not wait for morning, so she could watch them again.

eng-g02-t02-u06-cq011 · unit 6 · Short answer · Amal and the Little Garden Friends

What did Adam say a butterfly uses its antennae for?

answer: To feel and to smell.

Adam explains the butterfly first, naming its six legs, its two antennae and what the antennae do.

the text it is asked about
Early one warm morning, Amal went out to the garden behind her house. The sun was soft and gold. Birds sang from the tall tree by the wall. The air smelled of wet soil after the rain.
Amal loved the garden. But there was one thing she did not love: bugs. When a bee buzzed near her, she ran. When she saw a spider, she covered her eyes.
Her older brother Adam was already there. He sat very still on a low rock.
“Come, Amal,” he whispered. “Sit with me. If you look closely, the garden is full of little friends.”
Amal sat down slowly. On a bright red flower, a butterfly opened and closed its wings.
“Look,” said Adam. “A butterfly is an insect. It has six legs, and two antennae on its head. Its antennae help it feel and smell. It drinks sweet nectar from the flower.”
The butterfly did not seem scary at all. Amal smiled a little.
Then a bee flew above the flowers, going from one to the next.
“Is the bee going to sting me?” Amal asked, pulling back.
“No,” said Adam gently. “The bee is very busy. It is collecting nectar to make honey. Stay calm and do not wave your arms. Then the bee will leave you alone.”
Amal held still. The bee buzzed near her hand, then flew away. She let out a happy breath. She was not afraid now.
Near her foot, a long line of ants marched over the soil. They were carrying tiny seeds.
“Where are they going?” Amal asked.
“To their home,” said Adam. “Ants live in a big anthill under the ground. They work together and share everything. One ant is small, but many ants are very strong.”
Amal watched the ants for a long time. She liked how they helped each other, just like a big family.
Then she saw something between two branches of the small lemon tree. It was a web, shining with drops of water. In the middle sat a spider.
Amal wanted to run, but she did not.
“A spider is not an insect,” said Adam. “Count its legs.”
Amal counted, one by one. “Eight legs!” she said. “Insects have only six.”
“That is right,” said Adam, proud of her. “A spider spins its web from silk in its own body. The web catches insects for the spider to eat. It is not here to hurt you. It is only doing its work.”
Amal looked at the web. It was beautiful, like tiny threads of glass.
Just then, their little sister Mina came running. She lifted her foot to stamp on a fat worm on the path.
“Wait!” called Amal. “Do not step on it. A worm has no legs, but it is a good little animal. Worms crawl through the soil and help our plants grow strong.”
Mina stopped. Together, the three children watched the worm wriggle safely under a leaf.
That evening, Amal told her mother everything. “I used to be afraid of bugs,” she said. “But now I know they are little friends. The bee makes honey and the ants work together. The spider spins a web and the worm helps the soil.”
Her mother smiled. “When we understand something,” she said, “we stop being afraid of it. And when we care for even the smallest creature, our hearts grow bigger.”
Amal looked out at the dark garden. All her little friends were resting. She could not wait for morning, so she could watch them again.

Adam answers Amal's worried question about stinging with two clear pieces of advice.

How did Amal know that the spider was not an insect?

answer: She counted its legs one by one and found eight, and insects have only six.

Adam asked her to count the legs, and her own words in the next line show what she found.

How did Amal change from the beginning of the story to the end? What helped her change?

answer: She began afraid of bugs and ended calling them her little friends. Watching them closely with Adam, and having each creature explained, helped her change.

Amal's mother said, “When we understand something, we stop being afraid of it.” Tell about something you were afraid of before, but are not afraid of now.

answer: Any personal example that names the thing, says what helped you understand it, and says how you feel about it now.

This question links the mother's words to your own life. Try to say what helped, just as Adam helped Amal.

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq001 · unit 7 · Short answer · Mother Earth Day

What is Mother Earth Day?

answer: It is a special day when people all around the world show that they care about our planet.

The very first sentence of the text explains what the day is and who takes part in it.

the text it is asked about
Read this story:

Mother Earth Day is a special day when people all around the world show that they care about our planet. Teacher Yasmin explained this to her class on a warm morning in April.

“Mother Earth Day is coming on Saturday,” said Teacher Yasmin. “On this day, people everywhere do good things for our planet. What could we do?”

Amal put up her hand first. “We could plant trees and flowers,” she said, “because trees and plants help clean the air we breathe.”

“That is a wonderful idea,” said Teacher Yasmin. “What else could we try?”

Adam thought for a moment. “We could pick up litter from parks, beaches and streets,” he said. “That keeps the Earth clean and safe for animals.”

Nora smiled. “My family is learning about recycling,” she said. “Recycling helps save trees and water, so we do not need to cut down so many trees.”

“Three excellent ideas,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Planting, picking up litter and recycling. Let’s do all three this Saturday.”

On Mother Earth Day, the whole class met at the park with their families. Amal and Adam planted six small trees along the path. Nora and her mother filled two bags with litter from beside the pond. Theo sorted old bottles and cans into the recycling bins.

By the afternoon, the park looked green and tidy. Birds landed in the new trees, and the path was clear of rubbish.

“You have all worked so hard today,” said Teacher Yasmin, looking around at the class. “But remember, we do not have to wait for one special day. Every day can be Earth Day if we remember to care for our planet.”

Amal nodded. “I am going to pick up litter every week, not just today,” she said.

“Me too,” said Adam. “And I am going to water our new trees every morning on my way to school.”

That evening, Amal told her grandmother Hana all about the trees, the litter and the recycling. “It was a busy day,” said Hana, “but a good one. The Earth says thank you.”

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq002 · unit 7 · Short answer · Mother Earth Day

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq003 · unit 7 · Short answer · Mother Earth Day

The text gives one reason for planting trees and flowers. What is it?

answer: Trees and plants help clean the air we breathe.

The word because in the text points straight to the reason.

Adam thought for a moment. “We could pick up litter from parks, beaches and streets,” he said. “That keeps the Earth clean and safe for animals.”

Nora smiled. “My family is learning about recycling,” she said. “Recycling helps save trees and water, so we do not need to cut down so many trees.”

“Three excellent ideas,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Planting, picking up litter and recycling. Let’s do all three this Saturday.”

On Mother Earth Day, the whole class met at the park with their families. Amal and Adam planted six small trees along the path. Nora and her mother filled two bags with litter from beside the pond. Theo sorted old bottles and cans into the recycling bins.

By the afternoon, the park looked green and tidy. Birds landed in the new trees, and the path was clear of rubbish.

“You have all worked so hard today,” said Teacher Yasmin, looking around at the class. “But remember, we do not have to wait for one special day. Every day can be Earth Day if we remember to care for our planet.”

Amal nodded. “I am going to pick up litter every week, not just today,” she said.

“Me too,” said Adam. “And I am going to water our new trees every morning on my way to school.”

That evening, Amal told her grandmother Hana all about the trees, the litter and the recycling. “It was a busy day,” said Hana, “but a good one. The Earth says thank you.”

Why does the text say picking up litter matters?

answer: It keeps the Earth clean and safe for animals.

The sentence about parks, beaches and streets ends with the reason for the work.

the text it is asked about
Read this story:

Mother Earth Day is a special day when people all around the world show that they care about our planet. Teacher Yasmin explained this to her class on a warm morning in April.

“Mother Earth Day is coming on Saturday,” said Teacher Yasmin. “On this day, people everywhere do good things for our planet. What could we do?”

Amal put up her hand first. “We could plant trees and flowers,” she said, “because trees and plants help clean the air we breathe.”

“That is a wonderful idea,” said Teacher Yasmin. “What else could we try?”

Adam thought for a moment. “We could pick up litter from parks, beaches and streets,” he said. “That keeps the Earth clean and safe for animals.”

Nora smiled. “My family is learning about recycling,” she said. “Recycling helps save trees and water, so we do not need to cut down so many trees.”

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq005 · unit 7 · Short answer · Mother Earth Day

Can Earth Day be only one day a year? Use what Teacher Yasmin says at the end of the day to explain your answer.

answer: No. Teacher Yasmin says that every day can be Earth Day if we remember to care for our planet.

This question asks you to find what the text says and turn it into your own answer.

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq006 · unit 7 · Short answer · Plants and Clean Air

Why does the text say plants are very important for our planet?

answer: Their green leaves clean the dirty air, so the air we breathe stays good.

The text names the job that green leaves do for the whole planet.

the text it is asked about
Read this story about plants and the air we breathe.

Teacher Yasmin took her class outside to sit under the big trees beside the school garden. “Today we are going to find out why plants are so important for our planet,” she said.

“I know plants are pretty,” said Nora, “but why are they important?”

“The green leaves of trees and plants clean the dirty air,” said Teacher Yasmin. “They take in a gas called carbon dioxide, and they give out a gas called oxygen. Oxygen is what we need to breathe.”

Theo looked up at the tall tree above him. “So this tree is breathing out air for us right now?” he asked.

“That is exactly right,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Big forests do this job on a huge scale. That is why people call forests the lungs of the Earth — they help the Earth breathe, just like your lungs help you breathe.”

Amal thought about her own little tree at home. “Does a small plant do the same job as a big tree?” she asked.

“Yes, just a smaller amount,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Even the plants inside our homes and classrooms clean the air around them, and they make our rooms more beautiful too.”

Adam pointed at the pots of flowers on the classroom windowsill. “Is that why we have plants inside our classroom?” he asked.

“Yes,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Those plants are working quietly all day, cleaning our air while we learn.”

After the lesson, the children walked around the garden looking for every plant they could find. They counted trees, bushes, flowers and even the moss on the old wall.

“There are so many plants helping us,” said Nora, writing the number in her notebook.

“That is why taking care of plants is a way of taking care of ourselves,” said Teacher Yasmin. “When we water them, protect them and plant new ones, we are also protecting our own air.”

On the way home, Amal told her grandmother Hana what she had learned. “Plants are like the Earth’s lungs,” she said proudly.

Hana smiled. “Then we must always look after them well,” she said, “so the Earth can keep breathing for all of us.”

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq009 · unit 7 · Short answer · Plants and Clean Air

The text says that taking care of plants is taking care of ourselves. Explain why that is true.

answer: Plants give out the oxygen we need and clean the air in our homes and schools, so looking after them keeps us well.

You have to join two ideas from the text: what plants give out, and where they clean the air.

the text it is asked about

Teacher Yasmin took her class outside to sit under the big trees beside the school garden. “Today we are going to find out why plants are so important for our planet,” she said.

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq010 · unit 7 · Short answer · A Family on Mother Earth Day

What is Sami’s father doing, and who is helping him?

answer: He is planting trees, and his uncle is helping him.

The family text names both the action and the helper.

the text it is asked about
Listen to this story about Sami’s family, then read it again yourself.

Today is Mother Earth Day, and Sami’s family is busy from morning until evening. Sami sits on the porch, watching everyone work.

In the garden, Sami’s father is planting trees with his uncle. They dig small holes in the soft soil and place a young tree in each one.

“How many trees are we planting today?” asks Sami’s uncle.

“Six trees,” says Sami’s father. “One for every year since we moved to this house.”

Near the fence, Sami’s mother is counting birds with his aunt. They sit very still with a notebook and a pencil.

“I can see three small brown birds in that bush,” whispers his aunt.

“Write that down,” says Sami’s mother. “We are helping to find out which birds live in our garden, so we know how to keep them safe.”

Down by the gate, Sami’s brother is picking up litter with his friends. They fill two big bags with old bottles, paper and bags that blew in from the street.

“This bag is nearly full,” says one friend.

“Good,” says Sami’s brother. “A clean street means a happy street.”

Inside the house, Sami is making paper flowers with his grandmother. She shows him how to fold old newspapers into soft petals.

“Can we really make flowers from old newspaper?” asks Sami.

“Yes,” says his grandmother. “Nothing needs to go to waste. We can turn old paper into something new and pretty.”

By the evening, the family gathers in the garden to look at their work: six new trees, a page full of bird names, two full bags of litter, and a bowl of paper flowers on the table.

“We have done so much today,” says Sami’s father, looking around proudly.

“Let’s promise something,” says Sami’s mother. “Let’s not wait for Mother Earth Day to come around again.”

The whole family agrees together: they promise to do something good for the Earth every day, not only on Mother Earth Day.

Sami smiles and holds up his favourite paper flower. “Every day can be a flower day,” he says, and everyone laughs.

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq013 · unit 7 · Short answer · A Poem for the Earth

Which two colours does the poem name, and what is each colour for?

answer: Blue is for the sky and green is for the Earth.

The first two lines of the poem pair one colour with one thing each.

the text it is asked about
Listen, then read along:
I’m glad the sky is painted blue,
And Earth is painted green,
With such a lot of nice fresh air
All sandwiched in between.

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq014 · unit 7 · Short answer · A Poem for the Earth

What does the poet say is “sandwiched in between” the sky and the Earth?

answer: A lot of nice fresh air.

The last two lines describe the air as if it were the filling in a sandwich.

the text it is asked about
Listen, then read along:
I’m glad the sky is painted blue,
And Earth is painted green,
With such a lot of nice fresh air
All sandwiched in between.

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq015 · unit 7 · Short answer · Amal and the Little Tree

What did Amal and Adam find all along the path to the river?

answer: They found litter: old bags and papers lying in the soil.

The second paragraph of the story describes exactly what Amal saw on the hot day.

the text it is asked about
Amal lived in a small village beside a river. She loved to sit under the old tree by her house. Its green leaves gave her cool shade, and birds sang in its branches every morning.
One hot day, Amal saw litter all along the path to the river. Old bags and papers lay in the soil.
“Look at all this litter,” said Amal to her brother Adam. “Our village is not clean.”
“You are right,” said Adam. “Let us pick it up.”
Amal and Adam spent the morning picking up the litter. They put the paper in a box for recycling. Their friend Nora came to help too.
“When we recycle this paper,” said Nora, “fewer trees are cut down. Recycling helps the Earth.”
Soon the path was clean. Amal felt happy. “I’m glad that our village looks beautiful again,” she said.
But Amal looked sad when she saw the river. It was almost dry. Along the bank, where tall trees once grew, the land was empty and brown.
“Where did all the trees go?” asked Amal.
Her grandmother, Hana, was sitting nearby. “People cut them down for wood,” she said softly. “Without the trees, the sun dried the earth, and the river grew small. Trees clean the air and hold the water in the soil.”
“Then we must plant new trees!” said Amal.
Grandmother Hana smiled. She gave Amal a handful of small seeds. “Here,” she said. “Little things can make a big difference.”
The next morning, Amal and Adam dug small holes in the soft soil. Theo, the boy from next door, ran over to watch.
“What are you doing?” asked Theo.
“We are planting seeds,” said Amal. “These seeds will grow into tall trees one day.”
“Can I help?” asked Theo.
“Yes! You can be my partner,” said Amal, laughing.
Every morning, Amal was watering the little seeds. She watched the soil carefully. One day, a tiny green stem pushed up from the ground. Then came two small leaves.
“Look, Adam!” cried Amal. “The roots are growing under the ground, and the stem is growing up to the sky!”
The children looked after their plants all through the dry season. Theo carried water. Nora pulled out the weeds. Slowly, the little trees grew taller and stronger.
After many months, one small tree grew its very first flower. It was white and soft, and it smelled sweet.
“Our tree has a flower!” said Amal. “Soon there will be seeds, and we can plant even more trees.”
The whole village came to look. The children had planted a row of little trees along the river bank. Birds began to visit again. The air smelled fresh and clean.
Grandmother Hana put her hand on Amal’s shoulder. “You cared for the Earth,” she said, “and the Earth is saying thank you.”
That night, Amal looked at the young trees under the moon. “I’m thankful for the trees, the water and the clean air,” she whispered. “I promise to take care of you always.”
And every day after that, Amal remembered her grandmother’s words: little things can make a big difference.

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq016 · unit 7 · Short answer · Amal and the Little Tree

“Look, Adam!” cried Amal. “The roots are growing under the ground, and the stem is growing up to the sky!”
The children looked after their plants all through the dry season. Theo carried water. Nora pulled out the weeds. Slowly, the little trees grew taller and stronger.
After many months, one small tree grew its very first flower. It was white and soft, and it smelled sweet.
“Our tree has a flower!” said Amal. “Soon there will be seeds, and we can plant even more trees.”
The whole village came to look. The children had planted a row of little trees along the river bank. Birds began to visit again. The air smelled fresh and clean.
Grandmother Hana put her hand on Amal’s shoulder. “You cared for the Earth,” she said, “and the Earth is saying thank you.”
That night, Amal looked at the young trees under the moon. “I’m thankful for the trees, the water and the clean air,” she whispered. “I promise to take care of you always.”
And every day after that, Amal remembered her grandmother’s words: little things can make a big difference.

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq018 · unit 7 · Short answer · Amal and the Little Tree

What did Grandmother Hana give Amal, and what did she say about little things?

answer: She gave her a handful of small seeds and said that little things can make a big difference.

This moment starts the whole planting plan, and the same words return at the end of the story.

the text it is asked about
Amal lived in a small village beside a river. She loved to sit under the old tree by her house. Its green leaves gave her cool shade, and birds sang in its branches every morning.
One hot day, Amal saw litter all along the path to the river. Old bags and papers lay in the soil.
“Look at all this litter,” said Amal to her brother Adam. “Our village is not clean.”
“You are right,” said Adam. “Let us pick it up.”
Amal and Adam spent the morning picking up the litter. They put the paper in a box for recycling. Their friend Nora came to help too.
“When we recycle this paper,” said Nora, “fewer trees are cut down. Recycling helps the Earth.”
Soon the path was clean. Amal felt happy. “I’m glad that our village looks beautiful again,” she said.
But Amal looked sad when she saw the river. It was almost dry. Along the bank, where tall trees once grew, the land was empty and brown.
“Where did all the trees go?” asked Amal.
Her grandmother, Hana, was sitting nearby. “People cut them down for wood,” she said softly. “Without the trees, the sun dried the earth, and the river grew small. Trees clean the air and hold the water in the soil.”
“Then we must plant new trees!” said Amal.
Grandmother Hana smiled. She gave Amal a handful of small seeds. “Here,” she said. “Little things can make a big difference.”
The next morning, Amal and Adam dug small holes in the soft soil. Theo, the boy from next door, ran over to watch.
“What are you doing?” asked Theo.
“We are planting seeds,” said Amal. “These seeds will grow into tall trees one day.”
“Can I help?” asked Theo.
“Yes! You can be my partner,” said Amal, laughing.
Every morning, Amal was watering the little seeds. She watched the soil carefully. One day, a tiny green stem pushed up from the ground. Then came two small leaves.
“Look, Adam!” cried Amal. “The roots are growing under the ground, and the stem is growing up to the sky!”
The children looked after their plants all through the dry season. Theo carried water. Nora pulled out the weeds. Slowly, the little trees grew taller and stronger.
After many months, one small tree grew its very first flower. It was white and soft, and it smelled sweet.
“Our tree has a flower!” said Amal. “Soon there will be seeds, and we can plant even more trees.”
The whole village came to look. The children had planted a row of little trees along the river bank. Birds began to visit again. The air smelled fresh and clean.
Grandmother Hana put her hand on Amal’s shoulder. “You cared for the Earth,” she said, “and the Earth is saying thank you.”
That night, Amal looked at the young trees under the moon. “I’m thankful for the trees, the water and the clean air,” she whispered. “I promise to take care of you always.”
And every day after that, Amal remembered her grandmother’s words: little things can make a big difference.

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq019 · unit 7 · Short answer · Amal and the Little Tree

Name the parts of the plant that Amal saw growing, starting with the roots.

answer: Roots, then the stem, then the leaves, and many months later the flower.

The story shows the parts in growing order, so read those paragraphs one after the other.

How did the village change after the children planted the trees?

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq021 · unit 7 · Short answer · Amal and the Little Tree

Grandmother Hana said, “Little things can make a big difference.” What little thing did the children do, and what big difference did it make?

answer: They planted and watered a handful of small seeds. Those seeds grew into a row of trees that brought the birds back and made the air fresh and clean.

Match the small action at the start of the story to the large change at the end.

What is one small thing you could do this week to care for the world around you?

eng-g02-t03-u07-cq023 · unit 7 · Short answer · Amal and the Little Tree

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq001 · unit 8 · Short answer · A Poem: “Homes”

Which three animal homes does the poem name, and which animal lives in each one?

answer: A nest is for a bird, a hive is for a bee, and a hole is for a rabbit.

Lines one, two and three each name one animal and its home, so read them in order to collect all three pairs.

the text it is asked about
Read this little poem out loud:
A nest is a home for a bird.
A hive is a home for a bee.
A hole is a home for a rabbit.
And a house is a home for me.

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq002 · unit 8 · Short answer · A Poem: “Homes”

In the last line of the poem, what kind of home does the speaker live in?

answer: A house.

The final line says, “And a house is a home for me.”, so the speaker's own home is a house.

the text it is asked about
Read this little poem out loud:
A nest is a home for a bird.
A hive is a home for a bee.
A hole is a home for a rabbit.
And a house is a home for me.

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq003 · unit 8 · Short answer · A Poem: “Homes”

The poem repeats the same sentence pattern in every line. Write that pattern with two blanks.

answer: A ______ is a home for a ______.

Poems often repeat a pattern. Here the home comes first and the animal comes last in every line, which makes the poem easy to join in with.

the text it is asked about
Read this little poem out loud:
A nest is a home for a bird.
A hive is a home for a bee.
A hole is a home for a rabbit.
And a house is a home for me.

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq004 · unit 8 · Short answer · Different Kinds of Homes

Name two kinds of homes that the text says people live in.

answer: Any two of: flats, houses with a garden, adobe houses, houses on stilts.

The text names four kinds of home in turn. Two of them are enough.

the text it is asked about
Read this text:
Teacher Yasmin held up a big picture book called Homes Around the World. “People live in many different kinds of homes,” she told the class. “Let’s look at some together.”
She pointed to the first picture. “This is a city street,” she said. “Look how tall the buildings are.”
“I live in a building like that!” said Leo, waving his hand. “My home is a flat. A flat is a set of rooms that is part of a bigger building. My flat is on the fifth floor, so I ride the lift every day.”
“Thank you, Leo,” said Teacher Yasmin. She turned the page. “Some people live in a house instead, with a garden all around it.”
“That sounds like my home,” said Amal. “My house has a small garden. My little brother Idris and I like to play there after school.”
Teacher Yasmin turned to the next picture. It showed thick brown walls and a flat roof, under a bright yellow sun. “In some hot, dry places, people build adobe houses,” she said. “An adobe house is made from mud and dry grass. Its walls are very thick.”
“Why are the walls so thick?” asked Nora.
“Good question,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Thick mud walls keep the inside of the house cool, even when it is very hot outside.”
She turned the page again. This picture showed houses standing high above the water, on long wooden legs. “In some hot, wet places, people build houses on stilts,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Stilts lift the house up into the air.”
“Why do they need to be so high up?” asked Sami.
“So that air can blow underneath the house and keep it cool,” said Teacher Yasmin. “It also keeps the house dry if the ground floods.”
Teacher Yasmin closed the book. “A house, a flat, an adobe house, a house on stilts — every home looks different,” she said, “but every home does the same important job. It keeps a family safe, warm and dry.”
“I want to visit a house on stilts one day,” said Leo.
“Me too!” said Amal. “But tonight, I am happy to go home to my own house, with my own garden.”
The whole class laughed, and Teacher Yasmin smiled. “That is exactly right,” she said. “Every home is special, because it is yours.”

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq006 · unit 8 · Short answer · Different Kinds of Homes

What are adobe houses built from, and why do their thick walls help in a hot, dry place?

answer: They are built from mud and dry grass, and the thick mud walls keep the rooms cool.

Both halves of the answer sit in the same two sentences: one gives the material and the next gives the reason.

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq007 · unit 8 · Short answer · Different Kinds of Homes

In hot, wet places, why do people build their houses high on stilts?

answer: So that the air can blow underneath the house.

The text gives the reason straight after the fact, using the word so, which signals why something is done.

the text it is asked about
Read this text:
Teacher Yasmin held up a big picture book called Homes Around the World. “People live in many different kinds of homes,” she told the class. “Let’s look at some together.”
She pointed to the first picture. “This is a city street,” she said. “Look how tall the buildings are.”

Teacher Yasmin turned to the next picture. It showed thick brown walls and a flat roof, under a bright yellow sun. “In some hot, dry places, people build adobe houses,” she said. “An adobe house is made from mud and dry grass. Its walls are very thick.”
“Why are the walls so thick?” asked Nora.
“Good question,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Thick mud walls keep the inside of the house cool, even when it is very hot outside.”
She turned the page again. This picture showed houses standing high above the water, on long wooden legs. “In some hot, wet places, people build houses on stilts,” said Teacher Yasmin. “Stilts lift the house up into the air.”
“Why do they need to be so high up?” asked Sami.
“So that air can blow underneath the house and keep it cool,” said Teacher Yasmin. “It also keeps the house dry if the ground floods.”
Teacher Yasmin closed the book. “A house, a flat, an adobe house, a house on stilts — every home looks different,” she said, “but every home does the same important job. It keeps a family safe, warm and dry.”
“I want to visit a house on stilts one day,” said Leo.
“Me too!” said Amal. “But tonight, I am happy to go home to my own house, with my own garden.”
The whole class laughed, and Teacher Yasmin smiled. “That is exactly right,” she said. “Every home is special, because it is yours.”

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq008 · unit 8 · Short answer · Theo's Tree House

Who built Theo's tree house, and where did he build it?

answer: Theo's father built it, and it is in their garden.

Theo says it himself, naming both the builder and the place.

the text it is asked about
Listen to Theo. Ask your AI tutor to read it aloud, or read it aloud yourself.
“My name is Theo. Let me tell you about my tree house! Last spring, my father said, ‘Theo, I have an idea for the garden.’ He worked every weekend for a whole month. First, he built a strong floor between two thick branches. Then he built four walls, a little door and a small window. Last of all, he painted a green roof, so the tree house matches the leaves.
Now the tree house is finished, and I love it! To get up, I climb a wooden ladder. It has ten steps, and I always count them: one, two, three, all the way to ten. Inside, there is a small shelf for my books and a soft cushion to sit on. The tree house has two floors. On the bottom floor, my friend Sami and I read comics together. On the top floor, there is just enough room to lie down and look out of the window at the sky.
‘Can we come up too?’ asked Amal and Idris one afternoon. ‘Of course!’ I said. ‘But climb slowly, one step at a time.’ We all sat together on the bottom floor and shared a bag of dried apricots. From the window, we could see the whole garden, the kitchen door, and even the roof of my house.
‘I wish I had a tree house,’ said Idris.
‘You can visit mine any time,’ I told him.
When it rains, I cannot play in the tree house, so I wait for a sunny day. My favourite time is the evening, when the sky turns orange and pink. I sit at the little window and watch the birds fly home to their nests.
My father says a tree house is like a small house in a tree, because it has walls, a roof and a window, just like a real house. But I think my tree house is even better than a real house, because it is up in the branches, close to the birds and the leaves and the sky.”

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq009 · unit 8 · Short answer · Theo's Tree House

How does Theo get up into his tree house?

answer: He climbs a ladder.

Theo explains, “To get up, I climb a wooden ladder.”, so a ladder is the way up.

Theo lists the three parts together, and they are the same parts that any house has.

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq011 · unit 8 · Short answer · Theo's Tree House

Would you like to have a tree house of your own? Give one reason for your answer.

answer: Any clear yes or no answer with one sensible reason, such as “Yes, because I could read up in the branches.”

The reason matters more than the choice. A good answer gives a full sentence that joins the opinion to a because.

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq012 · unit 8 · Short answer · Helping Hands at Home

Who is coming to visit Amal's family, and how long will she stay?

answer: Grandma Hana is coming, and she will stay for one whole week.

Mother's good news at the start of the story names the visitor and the length of the visit.

the text it is asked about
Amal lived in a small house near the market with her mother, her father, and her little brother Idris. The house had two bedrooms, a kitchen, a bathroom, and a cosy living room with a bright red rug on the floor.
One sunny morning, Mother had good news. “Grandma Hana will visit us tomorrow,” she said. “She will stay for one whole week!”
“Grandma is coming!” shouted Idris, jumping up and down.
Amal smiled, but then she looked around. There were toys under the sofa. There were dishes in the sink. There was dust on the little table by the window. The house was not ready for a special guest.
“Our home must look nice for Grandma,” said Mother. “But I cannot do it all by myself. Will you help me?”
“I will help!” said Amal.
“Me too!” said Idris.
Mother clapped her hands happily. “Then we are a team,” she said. “Let us start.”
First, they went to the bedroom. “I will make my bed,” said Amal. She pulled the blanket flat and put her pillow at the top. Then she put her clean clothes away in the wardrobe. Idris picked up his toys from under the bed and put them in the toy box.
“Look, Amal!” he said. “Now the floor is clear!”
Next, they went to the living room. Amal swept the floor with the big broom. She shook the red rug outside so that it was clean again. Idris wiped the little table until the dust was gone.
“Where should I put this book?” asked Idris.
“Put it on the shelf, next to the other books,” said Amal. Idris placed it neatly on the shelf.
In the kitchen, Mother was cooking sweet rice and warm vegetable pies for tomorrow. The kitchen smelled wonderful. Amal washed the dishes in the sink, and Idris dried them with a cloth. Then Amal set the table with clean plates and cups.
“You are working so hard,” said Father, coming in from the garden. “I have news too. Look outside the window!”
The children ran to the window. In the garden, under the big tree, Father had built something new. It had walls, a small roof, and a tiny window.
“A tree house!” cried Amal. “For us?”
“Yes,” laughed Father. “When your work is done, you may climb up and play. But first, we finish helping your mother.”
So Amal and Idris worked until every room was tidy. The bedroom was neat. The living room was clean. There were fresh flowers on the table, and the whole house felt warm and welcoming.
The next day, Grandma Hana arrived. She looked around the little house and smiled a big smile.
“What a beautiful home,” she said. “It is clean, it is warm, and it is full of love. Who helped to make it so lovely?”
“We did!” said Amal and Idris together.
Grandma Hana hugged them both. “A home is not just walls and a roof,” she said softly. “A home is a place where a family helps each other. Thank you, my dear helping hands.”
That evening, Amal and Idris climbed the ladder to their new tree house. They looked down at their little house, warm and bright below them.
“Home, sweet home,” said Amal happily. And she felt very glad that she had helped.

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq018 · unit 8 · Short answer · Helping Hands at Home

Grandma Hana called Amal and Idris her “helping hands”. Which two jobs do you do at home to be a helping hand?

answer: Any two real jobs named in full sentences, such as “At home, I set the table.” and “At home, I sweep the floor.”

A good answer names two real household jobs, each in a complete sentence.

the text it is asked about

“Me too!” said Idris.
Mother clapped her hands happily. “Then we are a team,” she said. “Let us start.”
First, they went to the bedroom. “I will make my bed,” said Amal. She pulled the blanket flat and put her pillow at the top. Then she put her clean clothes away in the wardrobe. Idris picked up his toys from under the bed and put them in the toy box.
“Look, Amal!” he said. “Now the floor is clear!”
Next, they went to the living room. Amal swept the floor with the big broom. She shook the red rug outside so that it was clean again. Idris wiped the little table until the dust was gone.
“Where should I put this book?” asked Idris.
“Put it on the shelf, next to the other books,” said Amal. Idris placed it neatly on the shelf.
In the kitchen, Mother was cooking sweet rice and warm vegetable pies for tomorrow. The kitchen smelled wonderful. Amal washed the dishes in the sink, and Idris dried them with a cloth. Then Amal set the table with clean plates and cups.
“You are working so hard,” said Father, coming in from the garden. “I have news too. Look outside the window!”
The children ran to the window. In the garden, under the big tree, Father had built something new. It had walls, a small roof, and a tiny window.
“A tree house!” cried Amal. “For us?”
“Yes,” laughed Father. “When your work is done, you may climb up and play. But first, we finish helping your mother.”
So Amal and Idris worked until every room was tidy. The bedroom was neat. The living room was clean. There were fresh flowers on the table, and the whole house felt warm and welcoming.
The next day, Grandma Hana arrived. She looked around the little house and smiled a big smile.
“What a beautiful home,” she said. “It is clean, it is warm, and it is full of love. Who helped to make it so lovely?”
“We did!” said Amal and Idris together.
Grandma Hana hugged them both. “A home is not just walls and a roof,” she said softly. “A home is a place where a family helps each other. Thank you, my dear helping hands.”
That evening, Amal and Idris climbed the ladder to their new tree house. They looked down at their little house, warm and bright below them.
“Home, sweet home,” said Amal happily. And she felt very glad that she had helped.

eng-g02-t03-u08-cq019 · unit 8 · Short answer · Helping Hands at Home

How did Amal feel at the end of the story, and why do you think helping made her feel that way?

answer: She felt very glad. Any reason grounded in the story, such as the house looked lovely, Grandma was happy, or the family worked as a team.

The last sentence gives the feeling. A good answer also gives a reason that links her gladness to the helping she did.

the text it is asked about
Amal lived in a small house near the market with her mother, her father, and her little brother Idris. The house had two bedrooms, a kitchen, a bathroom, and a cosy living room with a bright red rug on the floor.
One sunny morning, Mother had good news. “Grandma Hana will visit us tomorrow,” she said. “She will stay for one whole week!”
“Grandma is coming!” shouted Idris, jumping up and down.
Amal smiled, but then she looked around. There were toys under the sofa. There were dishes in the sink. There was dust on the little table by the window. The house was not ready for a special guest.
“Our home must look nice for Grandma,” said Mother. “But I cannot do it all by myself. Will you help me?”
“I will help!” said Amal.
“Me too!” said Idris.
Mother clapped her hands happily. “Then we are a team,” she said. “Let us start.”
First, they went to the bedroom. “I will make my bed,” said Amal. She pulled the blanket flat and put her pillow at the top. Then she put her clean clothes away in the wardrobe. Idris picked up his toys from under the bed and put them in the toy box.
“Look, Amal!” he said. “Now the floor is clear!”
Next, they went to the living room. Amal swept the floor with the big broom. She shook the red rug outside so that it was clean again. Idris wiped the little table until the dust was gone.
“Where should I put this book?” asked Idris.
“Put it on the shelf, next to the other books,” said Amal. Idris placed it neatly on the shelf.
In the kitchen, Mother was cooking sweet rice and warm vegetable pies for tomorrow. The kitchen smelled wonderful. Amal washed the dishes in the sink, and Idris dried them with a cloth. Then Amal set the table with clean plates and cups.
“You are working so hard,” said Father, coming in from the garden. “I have news too. Look outside the window!”
The children ran to the window. In the garden, under the big tree, Father had built something new. It had walls, a small roof, and a tiny window.
“A tree house!” cried Amal. “For us?”
“Yes,” laughed Father. “When your work is done, you may climb up and play. But first, we finish helping your mother.”
So Amal and Idris worked until every room was tidy. The bedroom was neat. The living room was clean. There were fresh flowers on the table, and the whole house felt warm and welcoming.
The next day, Grandma Hana arrived. She looked around the little house and smiled a big smile.
“What a beautiful home,” she said. “It is clean, it is warm, and it is full of love. Who helped to make it so lovely?”
“We did!” said Amal and Idris together.
Grandma Hana hugged them both. “A home is not just walls and a roof,” she said softly. “A home is a place where a family helps each other. Thank you, my dear helping hands.”
That evening, Amal and Idris climbed the ladder to their new tree house. They looked down at their little house, warm and bright below them.
“Home, sweet home,” said Amal happily. And she felt very glad that she had helped.

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq001 · unit 9 · Short answer · A Poem: “At the Zebra Crossing”

What does the poem tell you to look around at when you stand at the zebra crossing?

answer: City places and city faces.

Two lines of the poem begin with the same words, “Look around at”, and then name city places and city faces.

the text it is asked about
A zebra crossing is a safe place to cross the road. Read this poem:
At the zebra crossing,
Look around at city places.
Look around at city faces.
I bend down to tie my laces… Oops!
WALK! Hurry up! Let's cross!

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq002 · unit 9 · Short answer · A Poem: “At the Zebra Crossing”

What does the person in the poem stop to do just before crossing the road?

answer: The person bends down to tie their laces.

The fourth line says, “I bend down to tie my laces… Oops!”, so the laces are tied first.

the text it is asked about
A zebra crossing is a safe place to cross the road. Read this poem:
At the zebra crossing,
Look around at city places.
Look around at city faces.
I bend down to tie my laces… Oops!
WALK! Hurry up! Let's cross!

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq003 · unit 9 · Short answer · A Poem: “At the Zebra Crossing”

Which three words at the ends of the poem's lines rhyme with one another?

answer: Places, faces and laces.

All three words end with the same -aces sound, which is the rhyme pattern that holds the poem together.

the text it is asked about
A zebra crossing is a safe place to cross the road. Read this poem:
At the zebra crossing,
Look around at city places.
Look around at city faces.
I bend down to tie my laces… Oops!
WALK! Hurry up! Let's cross!

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq004 · unit 9 · Short answer · A Poem: “At the Zebra Crossing”

The last line is written in capital letters with exclamation marks. Why do you think the poet wrote it that way?

answer: Because the words are shouted, and shouting shows there is only a short, safe moment left to cross.

Capital letters and exclamation marks show a loud, urgent voice, and “Hurry up!” tells you the person must move quickly.

the text it is asked about
A zebra crossing is a safe place to cross the road. Read this poem:
At the zebra crossing,
Look around at city places.
Look around at city faces.
I bend down to tie my laces… Oops!
WALK! Hurry up! Let's cross!

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq005 · unit 9 · Short answer · The Aquarium Schedule

How many animal shows does the aquarium schedule list, and which animals can you see?

answer: Four shows: the octopus, the penguin, the shark and the turtle.

The schedule gives four times, and each time names one animal, so there are four shows in all.

the text it is asked about
On Sunday, Amal and Adam visited the city aquarium. At the front door, a big sign showed the schedule for the day.
“Let's read it together,” said Adam. “It tells us when to see the animals.”
Amal read the sign out loud.
Aquarium Schedule — Meet our amazing animals!
10:00 — Octopus Show (They're clever!)
11:00 — Penguin Feeding (They're beautiful!)
12:00 — Shark Feeding (They're hungry!)
2:00 — Turtle Time (They're huge!)
“It's only nine o'clock,” said Adam, looking at his watch. “We have one hour before the Octopus Show.”
“Let's walk around first,” said Amal. “I want to see the fish tanks.”
They walked past tanks full of small, colourful fish. Amal pressed her hand gently on the glass. “This aquarium is beautiful,” she said.
At ten o'clock, a keeper rang a bell. “The Octopus Show starts now!” she called.
Amal and Adam hurried to the big glass tank. The octopus moved its eight arms slowly through the water.
“Look how clever it is,” said Adam. “It can open a jar with its arms!”
“I don't like octopus food,” laughed Amal, “but I like watching it swim.”
At eleven o'clock, they found the penguins. The keeper threw small fish into the water, and the penguins jumped in after them.
“They're so beautiful when they swim,” said Amal. “But they look funny when they walk!”
Adam checked the schedule again. “Next is the Shark Feeding at twelve o'clock,” he said. “Sharks can be a little scary, but I want to see them.”
“Me too,” said Amal, “as long as the glass is strong!”
The shark feeding was loud and exciting. Everyone clapped when the biggest shark swam close to the glass.
“That was amazing!” said Amal.
Last of all, at two o'clock, they visited Turtle Time. A huge turtle swam slowly around its tank, and a keeper explained how old it was.
“One hundred years old!” said Adam. “That turtle is older than Grandpa!”
Amal laughed. “I'm glad we read the schedule,” she said. “Now we didn't miss a single show.”
“Next time,” said Adam, “we should come back and read it again — maybe there's a new animal to meet.”

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq006 · unit 9 · Short answer · The Aquarium Schedule

What time does the Penguin Feeding start, and what time does Turtle Time start?

answer: The Penguin Feeding starts at 11:00 and Turtle Time starts at 2:00.

The schedule lists the times down the left-hand side, so you read across from each time to find the animal.

If you arrive at the aquarium at 11:30, which show comes next?

answer: The Shark Feeding at 12:00.

11:30 is after the 11:00 Penguin Feeding, so the next time on the schedule is 12:00.

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq009 · unit 9 · Short answer · The City from the Sky

What does Leo see on the roads, and which tall building does the pilot point out?

answer: Leo sees all the traffic on the roads, and the tall building is the library.

Leo shouts about the traffic first, and the pilot answers by naming the library as the tall building.

the text it is asked about
Leo and his dad were going on a special trip. Today, they were going to ride in a helicopter above the city!
“Are you ready?” asked Dad, buckling Leo's seatbelt. “Hold on tight — we're going up!”
The helicopter lifted off the ground, and Leo's stomach felt light and bouncy.
“Wow!” said Leo. “Everything looks so small already!”
The pilot smiled and pointed out of the window. “From up here, you can see the whole city,” she said. “What can you find?”
Leo looked down. Long lines of cars moved slowly along the roads. “Look at all the traffic!” he said. “The cars look like tiny toys.”
“That's right,” said the pilot. “And do you see that tall building with all the windows?”
“Yes!” said Leo. “Is that the library?”
“It is,” said the pilot. “And next to it is the shopping centre, with the round glass roof.”
“I love the library,” said Leo, “but I love the shopping centre too.”
“I like shopping there,” said Dad, laughing.
“I don't!” said Leo. “I always get lost inside!”
The helicopter turned gently, and a wide blue river came into view.
“Look, Dad — the river!” said Leo. “And there's a boat on it.”
“That's the ferry,” said the pilot. “It takes people across the river every day, just like a bus takes people along the roads.”
“I want to ride the ferry one day,” said Leo.
Further along, Leo spotted a great wheel turning slowly in a park.
“The Ferris wheel!” he shouted. “It looks amazing from up here!”
“It's huge, isn't it?” said Dad. “Almost as tall as we are flying now.”
Near the park, Leo noticed rows of colourful stalls. “Is that the market?” he asked.
“Good eye,” said the pilot. “People buy fruit, fish and cloth there every morning.”
Leo pressed his face close to the window. “I can even see the aquarium,” he said, “with the blue roof shaped like a wave.”
“Maybe we'll visit the octopus and the penguins later,” said Dad.
“Yes, please!” said Leo.
As the helicopter turned back towards home, the pilot gave Leo a small map of the city. “Can you find your street on here?” she asked.
Leo studied the map carefully. “It's straight ahead from the library,” he said, “then turn left near the market.”
“Well done,” said the pilot. “You're a clever navigator.”
Leo grinned and looked out of the window one last time. The whole city glowed gold in the afternoon sun.
“This was the best day ever,” he said. “The city looks so beautiful from the sky.”

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq010 · unit 9 · Short answer · The City from the Sky

Leo's dad says, “I like shopping.” What does Leo reply, and what does his reply tell you?

answer: Leo replies, “I don't!”, which tells you that he does not enjoy shopping.

“I don't.” is the polite way to disagree, so father and son enjoy different things.

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq012 · unit 9 · Short answer · A Big Day in the City

How did Amal and Adam travel into the city on Saturday morning?

answer: They took the bus into the city.

The opening two sentences say that Amal took the bus and that Adam went with her.

the text it is asked about
On Saturday morning, Amal took the bus into the city. Her big brother Adam went with her. It was Amal's first big day of exploring. Her eyes were wide with wonder.
“Look at all the traffic!” said Amal. Cars and buses filled the roads. A helicopter flew high above the tall buildings.
“The city is amazing,” said Adam. “Come, I will show you the market first.”
The market was huge and busy. People were buying fish, fruit and bright cloth. Amal smelled fresh bread and sweet mangoes.
“I like the market,” said Amal happily.
“I do too!” said Adam. “Now let's go to the library. It is my favourite place.”
But near the shopping centre they saw an old man. He looked worried. He held a small map. He turned it this way and that.
“Excuse me,” the old man said kindly. “I am lost. Can you help me? I need to find the hospital. My daughter is there with a new baby.”
Amal looked at Adam. Their mother always said, “A kind person helps a stranger.”
“We can help you,” said Adam. “The hospital is not far.”
Adam pointed down the road. “Walk straight ahead to the traffic light,” he said. “Then turn left. Walk to the next street. The hospital is on your right. It is a big white building.”
The old man smiled. His face still looked a little worried. “Thank you,” he said. “But my legs are slow, and the traffic is scary.”
“Then we will walk with you,” said Amal.
So the three of them walked together. They crossed the road carefully at the zebra crossing. They walked straight ahead and turned left. There stood the hospital, a big white building. It was just as Adam had said.
“You are here!” said Amal.
“Thank you, my friends,” said the old man. His eyes were bright now. “You are clever and kind children. Today you helped a stranger. I will remember it for a long time.”
He waved goodbye and went inside to meet his new grandchild.
Amal felt warm inside. “Helping him was better than the market,” she said.
“I think so too,” laughed Adam. “Come — the library is close. And after that, I have a surprise.”
At the library, Amal read a beautiful book about the sea. She learned that an octopus has eight arms. She also learned that a turtle carries a hard shell.
Then came the surprise. Adam took her down to the river. They rode the ferry across the water. From the boat, Amal could see the whole city.
“Adam, look!” she cried. “I can see the Ferris wheel!”
The great wheel turned slowly against the blue sky. The city was beautiful.
That night, Amal told her mother everything. She talked about the huge market and the lost old man. She told her about the library book and the ferry ride.
“You had a big day,” her mother said, hugging her. “And you helped someone who needed you. That is the best thing of all.”
Amal closed her eyes and smiled. “Tomorrow,” she whispered, “I will explore the city again.”

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq013 · unit 9 · Short answer · A Big Day in the City

What did Amal smell at the market, and what were people buying there?

answer: She smelled fresh bread and sweet mangoes, and people were buying fish, fruit and bright cloth.

The market paragraph names what people bought and then what Amal smelled.

“Look at all the traffic!” said Amal. Cars and buses filled the roads. A helicopter flew high above the tall buildings.
“The city is amazing,” said Adam. “Come, I will show you the market first.”
The market was huge and busy. People were buying fish, fruit and bright cloth. Amal smelled fresh bread and sweet mangoes.
“I like the market,” said Amal happily.
“I do too!” said Adam. “Now let's go to the library. It is my favourite place.”
But near the shopping centre they saw an old man. He looked worried. He held a small map. He turned it this way and that.
“Excuse me,” the old man said kindly. “I am lost. Can you help me? I need to find the hospital. My daughter is there with a new baby.”
Amal looked at Adam. Their mother always said, “A kind person helps a stranger.”
“We can help you,” said Adam. “The hospital is not far.”
Adam pointed down the road. “Walk straight ahead to the traffic light,” he said. “Then turn left. Walk to the next street. The hospital is on your right. It is a big white building.”
The old man smiled. His face still looked a little worried. “Thank you,” he said. “But my legs are slow, and the traffic is scary.”
“Then we will walk with you,” said Amal.
So the three of them walked together. They crossed the road carefully at the zebra crossing. They walked straight ahead and turned left. There stood the hospital, a big white building. It was just as Adam had said.
“You are here!” said Amal.
“Thank you, my friends,” said the old man. His eyes were bright now. “You are clever and kind children. Today you helped a stranger. I will remember it for a long time.”
He waved goodbye and went inside to meet his new grandchild.
Amal felt warm inside. “Helping him was better than the market,” she said.
“I think so too,” laughed Adam. “Come — the library is close. And after that, I have a surprise.”
At the library, Amal read a beautiful book about the sea. She learned that an octopus has eight arms. She also learned that a turtle carries a hard shell.
Then came the surprise. Adam took her down to the river. They rode the ferry across the water. From the boat, Amal could see the whole city.
“Adam, look!” she cried. “I can see the Ferris wheel!”
The great wheel turned slowly against the blue sky. The city was beautiful.
That night, Amal told her mother everything. She talked about the huge market and the lost old man. She told her about the library book and the ferry ride.
“You had a big day,” her mother said, hugging her. “And you helped someone who needed you. That is the best thing of all.”
Amal closed her eyes and smiled. “Tomorrow,” she whispered, “I will explore the city again.”

What three directions did Adam give the old man to reach the hospital?

Adam had already given clear directions. Why did the children decide to walk with the old man as well?

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq019 · unit 9 · Short answer · A Big Day in the City

Amal said that helping the old man was better than the market. Why do you think she felt that way?

answer: There is more than one good answer. A good answer gives the idea that helping someone who needed her felt more important than shopping, and that it made her feel warm inside.

The story says Amal felt warm inside straight after she said it, which is the clue to her feeling.

eng-g02-t03-u09-cq020 · unit 9 · Short answer · A Big Day in the City

Their mother says, “A kind person helps a stranger.” What is one safe way you could help somebody who is lost near your home?

answer: There is more than one good answer. A safe idea such as telling a trusted adult, pointing to a landmark, or walking with the person only when an adult is with you.

A good answer names a helpful action and keeps a trusted adult involved.

Name two things in the story's city that you also have where you live, and one thing that you do not have.

The child must use words from the story rather than general ideas, which shows careful reading.

eng-g02-t03-u10-cq001 · unit 10 · Short answer · Amal's English Year

What did Amal find at the very front of her English folder?

answer: Her welcome card from Unit 1, with her name written in careful blue letters.

The first paragraph says the welcome card was at the front and the name was in careful blue letters.

the text it is asked about
Amal opened her English folder on the last Monday of Year 2. At the front she found her welcome card from Unit 1. Her name was written in careful blue letters. Behind it lay a poem about her neighbour Theo. Next to the poem was a drawing of Leila the firefighter.

Amal turned another page. She found her shadow chart from the big sky unit. She found a measuring table and a bug fact file. She found her garden promise as well. Last of all she found her city map from Unit 9.

Teacher Yasmin smiled at the class. “This term you will build one last project,” she said. “It is called My English World.” Every child had to choose six pages from at least four different units.

Amal read her old work again. She fixed one sentence on her helpers page. She had written “The firefighters is working.” Now she wrote “The firefighters are working.” She added two describing words to her sky page. Unit 4 had taught her about adjectives.

Then Amal made her new booklet. Page one showed a butterfly with its wings and antennae. Page two showed a helpful neighbour. Page three showed the parts of the body. Page four showed the sun and a long shadow. Page five showed the trees she helped to plant. Page six showed the library in the city.

On showcase day Amal stood beside her table. Her hands felt cold, so she breathed slowly. “Welcome to my English world,” she said. Nora and Leo listened, and Grandma Hana clapped.

At the end Amal wrote one last reflection. “I can read, write and speak more clearly now,” she wrote. “Next year I want to read a long chapter book.” She closed her folder and felt proud.

eng-g02-t03-u10-cq002 · unit 10 · Short answer · Amal's English Year

Which sentence did Amal correct on her helpers page, and what did she change it to?

answer: She had written “The firefighters is working.” She changed it to “The firefighters are working.”

Paragraph four quotes both versions, so you must copy the corrected verb are.

Teacher Yasmin smiled at the class. “This term you will build one last project,” she said. “It is called My English World.” Every child had to choose six pages from at least four different units.

Amal read her old work again. She fixed one sentence on her helpers page. She had written “The firefighters is working.” Now she wrote “The firefighters are working.” She added two describing words to her sky page. Unit 4 had taught her about adjectives.

Then Amal made her new booklet. Page one showed a butterfly with its wings and antennae. Page two showed a helpful neighbour. Page three showed the parts of the body. Page four showed the sun and a long shadow. Page five showed the trees she helped to plant. Page six showed the library in the city.

On showcase day Amal stood beside her table. Her hands felt cold, so she breathed slowly. “Welcome to my English world,” she said. Nora and Leo listened, and Grandma Hana clapped.

At the end Amal wrote one last reflection. “I can read, write and speak more clearly now,” she wrote. “Next year I want to read a long chapter book.” She closed her folder and felt proud.

eng-g02-t03-u10-cq003 · unit 10 · Short answer · Amal's English Year

Put these three steps in the order Amal did them: made the new booklet, read her old work again, closed her folder.

answer: She read her old work again, then she made the new booklet, then she closed her folder at the end.

The story moves in that order, from paragraph four to paragraph five and then to the last paragraph.

the text it is asked about
Amal opened her English folder on the last Monday of Year 2. At the front she found her welcome card from Unit 1. Her name was written in careful blue letters. Behind it lay a poem about her neighbour Theo. Next to the poem was a drawing of Leila the firefighter.

Amal turned another page. She found her shadow chart from the big sky unit. She found a measuring table and a bug fact file. She found her garden promise as well. Last of all she found her city map from Unit 9.

Amal wrote that she wants to read a long chapter book next year. What does that tell you about her reading now?

This is an inference: the goal only makes sense if her reading has already grown this year.
